in the language of price discrimination. For a fixed prior distribution, both seller-side segmentation and buyer-side information generate fixed-market payoff triangles. Once the market itself becomes an object of design, those triangles move with the market.
The next corollary shows that the relevant object is the common envelope of these
fixed-market triangles as the prior varies. In this sense, the linear-cost benchmark
identifies the endogenous limits of price discrimination.

To this end, for each
H\in\Delta([0,1])
, define efficient surplus by

W(H):=\bar{Q}\int_{M}^{1}S_{H}(v)\,dv.

Let
\Pi^{m}(H):=\bar{Q}\max_{r\in[M,1]}(r-M)S_{H}(r)
, and define the fixed-market
1
triangle by

T^{BBM}(H):=\{(c,\pi)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}:\ c\geq 0,\ \pi\geq\Pi^{m}(H),\ c+\pi\leq W(H)\}.

Also, let
\underline{\Pi}(H)
denote the seller-profit floor in the fixed-prior
buyer-information problem of
16
, and define the corresponding
fixed-market triangle by

T^{RS}(H):=\{(c,\pi)\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{2}:\ c\geq 0,\ \pi\geq\underline{\Pi}(H),\ c+\pi\leq W(H)\}.

Corollary 6
.

It holds that

\mathcal{V}^{pp}=\bigcup_{H\in\Delta([0,1])}T^{BBM}(H)=\bigcup_{H\in\Delta([0,1])}T^{RS}(H).

That is, once the prior distribution itself is allowed to vary, seller-side
segmentation and buyer-side information generate the same payoff region.

Taken together, Proposition
5
and Corollary
6
show that the posted price benchmark identifies the
endogenous limits of price discrimination in closed form. For a fixed prior, seller-side segmentation and
buyer-side information generate prior-specific payoff triangles. Once the prior itself is also chosen
upstream, those fixed-market distinctions no longer matter for the outer boundary: both families of
triangles generate the same envelope, namely
V^{pp}
.

The implication is the same as in the convex-cost model, but here it is completely transparent. Information and segmentation move outcomes within prior-specific regions; market
composition determines the envelope of those regions. In this sense, the linear-cost benchmark provides
the sharpest possible closed-form statement of the paper’s main message.

5
Richer Primitive Environments

Our main results assume that the upstream actor can choose any prior probability measure in the set of Borel probability measures on
[0,1]
. Thus, the upstream actor is essentially unrestricted in his choice of a market composition. In this section, we discuss and make precise the sense in which our main results hold for richer primitive environments with more restrictions on the set of feasible market compositions.

We first need to introduce some notation.
For two Borel probability measures
G,H
on
[0,1]
, we write
H\succ_{cx}G
if
H
dominates
G
in the convex-order, which is equivalent to
G
being a mean-preserving contraction of
H
, or, equivalently, to
H
being a mean-preserving spread of
G
. We let
MPS(G)
denote the set of mean-preserving spreads of
G
.

Moreover,given two two Borel probability measures
F,G
on
[0,1]
, we write

F\succeq_{icx}G
if

\int\psi(v)\,dF(v)\geq\int\psi(v)\,dG(v)

for every increasing convex function
\psi:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
. First-order
stochastic dominance implies increasing-convex order, but the converse need not hold.
8
8

8

We can think of the relation
F\succeq_{icx}G
as
F
being a mean-preserving spread of some
H
which in turn first-order stochastically dominates
G
, that is,
F\succeq_{icx}G
if and only if there exists
H
such that
F\succ H\succeq_{fosd}G
.

5.1
Fixed Average Valuation and Information Design

Maintain Assumptions
1
and
2
, and fix
k\in(1/2,1]
. Let

G_{k}
be the unique optimizer of the upstream actor’s problem. Recall that by our main structural results,

G_{k}
has support
\operatorname{supp}(G_{k})=[\underline{v}_{k},1]
, its lower quantile
Q_{k}
is strictly increasing on
(0,b_{k})
and satisfies
Q_{k}(u)=1
for all

u\in[b_{k},1]
, and
G_{k}
has a unique atom at
1
of size
1-b_{k}
.

We now discuss how the same object admits a hierarchy of interpretations. The least
constrained interpretation is the one built into the model: the upstream actor directly chooses the
effective market composition faced by the seller. The second, more restrictive, interpretation fixes only
the mean of the chosen distribution. The third, most restrictive, interpretation fixes a primitive prior and
allows the upstream actor to choose only among distributions that can be obtained from that prior as its
mean-preserving contractions. The results below show that the same law
G_{k}
solves all three problems.

Fixed Average Buyer Valuation.

Let
\mu_{k}:=\int_{[0,1]}x\,dG_{k}(x)
be the mean of
G_{k}
for a given
k
. Suppose the upstream actor is restricted to keeping the average consumer valuation fixed. Then
G_{k}
is the unique solution to the upstream actor’s problem under the fixed average valuation restriction. Therefore, the optimal market composition is not selected merely because it changes average demand. Even conditional on average willingness to
pay for quality, the upstream actor has a unique preferred way to shape the effective market composition faced by
the seller.

Information Design.

Suppose now consumers have a primitive prior
H
. The upstream actor is an information intermediary who provides information to consumers prior to trading. This is done by choosing an information structure whose realization is privately observed by the consumer. The choice of an information structure induces a distribution over posterior values, and we can think of the upstream actor’s problem as choosing directly this distribution.
For any prior
H\in\Delta([0,1])
, the feasible distributions of posterior means induced by an information structure are exactly
the mean-preserving contractions of
H
. Therefore, if
H
is a mean-preserving spread of
G_{k}
, then
G_{k}
is the unique maximizer of
\max\{W_{k}(G):H\succ_{cx}G\}
.

Note that the set of mean-preserving spreads of
G_{k}
given
k\in(1/2,1]
is a “large” set. Necessary conditions for a distribution
H
to be a mean-preserving spread of
G_{k}
include
\inf\operatorname{supp}H\leq\underline{v_{k}}
and having an atom at the highest valuation of size at least
1-b_{k}
.
9
9

9

Recall that
\underline{v_{k}}=\inf\operatorname{supp}(G_{k})
.
In the Online Appendix, we illustrate that
MPS(G_{k})
includes discrete distributions supported on
N
points for all integer
N\geq 2
, that is, the set of mean-preserving spreads of
G_{k}
given
k\in(1/2,1]
contains arbitrarily coarse finitely supported priors. Moreover, we show that it also contains smooth priors with an absolutely
continuous body and the same top atom. Figure
11
illustrates two such examples in the quadratic cost case (
k=1
), where the consumer-optimal market composition we identified remains optimal in the information design environment.

Figure 11
:
Consumer-Optimal Market Composition in the Information Design Environment

Therefore, the optimal market composition we identify is the market environment relevant for screening, even if we allow for certain richer primitive environments.

5.2
The Hold-Up Problem

We now investigate the robustness of the structural properties of the optimal market composition derived in Theorem
1
in the informational hold-up problem of
6
. A consumer who wishes to soften the seller’s screening problem may have to suppress or distort useful information about her willingness to pay. A regulator who wishes to broaden access may have to subsidize or compel the participation of weaker buyers. A platform or broker who wants to make the market less top-heavy may have to forego the easy targeting of premium demand. We model these frictions by allowing the upstream actor to incur a cost
C(G)
from choosing market composition
G
.

In our environment, the upstream actor manipulates not only dispersion but also the average valuation of the market composition. One market composition can differ from another one by having a higher mean, a thicker upper tail, or both. For this reason, we consider cost functions that are monotone in the increasing-convex order.

We consider the problem

\max_{\phi\in\Phi}\ \widehat{J}_{k}(\phi):=J_{k}(\phi)-C(G_{\phi}),\qquad k\in[0,1],

where
J_{k}
is the baseline upstream actor’s objective from the main model and establish that if costs are decreasing in increasing convex order, the structure of the optimal market composition is the one identified in Theorem
1
.

Assumption 3
.

The cost functional
C:\Delta([0,1])\to\mathbb{R}
is decreasing in increasing-convex
order: for any distributions
F
and
G
on
[0,1]
,

F\succeq_{icx}G
implies
C(F)\leq C(G)
.

In words, Assumption
3
says that making the market composition less top-heavy is costly. In particular, in the context of Proposition
1
, seller-friendly markets are cheaper to generate, while
consumer-friendly market compositions require costly intervention. This is the natural
“hold-up” analog of the costless model: the upstream actor still wants to
soften the market, but must now pay to do so.

The next proposition shows that the structural properties of the optimal market composition are fully preserved:
for low welfare weights, the degenerate top-type market remains optimal, and for high
welfare weights every maximizer remains fully active, exhibits a binding upper tail and features no bunching on the interior.

Proposition 6
.

Maintain Assumptions
1
and

3
. Suppose that
\phi^{*}\in\Phi
maximizes
\widehat{J}_{k}(\phi)
. Then,

1.

If
k\in[0,1/2]
, then
\phi^{*}(u)=1
for all
u\in[0,1]
.

2.

If
k\in(1/2,1]
, every solution
\phi^{*}
to the upstream actor’s problem has the following structure: there exists a (unique) cutoff
b\in[0,1)
such that
\phi^{*}
is strictly increasing on
(0,b)
with
0<\phi^{*}(u)<1
for all
u\in(0,b)
, and
\phi^{*}\equiv 1
on
[b,1]
.

Therefore, we can recover all three qualitative ingredients of the baseline
structure theorem: no exclusion, an efficient top tail, and full separation of the active
interior. We note that now we cannot in principle rule out the case that the degenerate
\delta_{1}
is optimal for any
k\in(1/2,1]
. However, this will be the case if for instance the optimizer of the costless baseline satisfies
\widehat{J}_{k}(\bar{\phi}_{k})>\widehat{J}_{k}(\mathbf{1})
.
10
10

10

To see this, note that by Proposition
6
, every maximizer
\phi^{*}
has an upper-tail cutoff
b\in[0,1)
such that
\phi^{*}(u)=1
for all
u\in[b,1]
. If
b=0
, then
\phi^{*}=\mathbf{1}
. But the hypothesis says that
\mathbf{1}
is not optimal, since
\widehat{J}_{k}(\bar{\phi}_{k})>\widehat{J}_{k}(\mathbf{1})
. Hence
b>0
.

The decreasing-in-icx case is the one most closely aligned with the baseline costless benchmark: although seller-friendlier market compositions are now costlier, the main qualitative structure survives. The opposite monotonicity case is different. If
C
is increasing in increasing-convex order, the regularization and truncation reductions need not be without loss, because they can raise the induced market in the
\succeq_{icx}
order and therefore raise the design cost. In the Online Appendix, we restrict attention to the reduced-form problem over
\Phi
and show that, for
k>1/2
, the top-type market is not optimal. Then we specialize the cost functional to the differentiable mean-based form
C(G)=\Gamma(\int v\,dG(v))
, and derive threshold restrictions on exclusion, bunching, and the emergence of a premium top segment.

6
Conclusion

In this paper, we show that the welfare effects of monopoly screening depend not only on how a seller screens and prices a given market but also on how upstream institutions shape the market composition the seller faces in the first place. By characterizing the optimal market composition and the efficient frontier of consumer surplus and profit, we provide a benchmark for how exposure, targeting, ranking, matching, disclosure, certification, and eligibility rules shape the division of surplus. The central implication is that improving consumer outcomes need not require suppressing differentiation. Instead, it requires reshaping the market so that screening becomes less favorable to the seller, which can be achieved by expanding the interior region in which information rents are generated and optimally shaping its composition, while preserving an efficient premium segment at the top. More broadly, the paper identifies market composition as a central determinant of the welfare consequences of monopoly screening.

Appendix: Proofs of the Main Results

Proof of Lemma
1

Proof.

We first verify that
\widetilde{Q}
is a valid quantile and that
\widetilde{G}\in\Omega
. Since
R
is a concave majorant of
\widehat{R}
, and
\widehat{R}\geq 0
, we have
R\geq 0
. Also, because
Q(u)\leq 1
, the affine function
u\mapsto 1-u
is a concave majorant of
\widehat{R}
. By minimality of
R=\operatorname{cav}(\widehat{R})
,

0\leq R(u)\leq 1-u
for all
u\in[0,1]
.
Hence

0\leq\widetilde{Q}(u)=\frac{R(u)}{1-u}\leq 1\qquad\forall u\in[0,1).

For
0\leq u<v<1
, concavity of
R
and
R(1)=0
imply

R(v)\geq\frac{1-v}{1-u}R(u)+\frac{v-u}{1-u}R(1)=\frac{1-v}{1-u}R(u)\Longleftrightarrow\frac{R(v)}{1-v}\geq\frac{R(u)}{1-u},

so
\widetilde{Q}
is nondecreasing on
[0,1)
. Since
R
is continuous on
[0,1]
,
\widetilde{Q}
is continuous on
[0,1)
. The limit

\widetilde{Q}(1):=\lim_{u\uparrow 1}\widetilde{Q}(u)

therefore exists in
[0,1]
. Thus
\widetilde{Q}
is a nondecreasing left-continuous map from
[0,1]
into
[0,1]
, and hence is the lower quantile of the distribution
\widetilde{G}
induced by
\widetilde{Q}(U)
, where
U\sim\operatorname{Unif}[0,1]
. Moreover,

(1-u)\widetilde{Q}(u)=R(u)

is concave, so
\widetilde{G}\in\Omega
.

The concavified revenue curve of
\widetilde{G}
is
R
, so
\widetilde{G}
has the same ironed virtual value
\phi
as
G
. Hence, by the seller-side reduction,
\Pi(\widetilde{G})=\Pi(G)
,
and
CS(\widetilde{G})-CS(G)=\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(\widetilde{Q}(u)-Q(u)\bigr)q(\phi(u))\,du
.
Since
R\geq\widehat{R}
,

\widetilde{Q}(u)-Q(u)=\frac{R(u)-\widehat{R}(u)}{1-u}\geq 0\qquad\forall u\in[0,1).

Therefore
CS(\widetilde{G})\geq CS(G)
, and so
W_{k}(\widetilde{G})\geq W_{k}(G)
. If
k>0
, then

W_{k}(\widetilde{G})-W_{k}(G)=k\int_{0}^{1}\bigl(\widetilde{Q}(u)-Q(u)\bigr)q(\phi(u))\,du,

so equality holds if and only if

\bigl(\widetilde{Q}(u)-Q(u)\bigr)q(\phi(u))=0

for Lebesgue-a.e.
u\in(0,1)
. The displayed supremum equality follows immediately.
∎

Proof of Lemma
2

Proof.

Since
\phi
is nondecreasing and right-continuous, so is

\phi^{+}(u):=\max\{\phi(u),0\}
.
Moreover,
0\leq\phi^{+}\leq 1
. Hence, by the preceding construction,
R^{+}
is concave,
Q^{+}
is the lower quantile of a distribution
G^{+}\in\Omega
, and the ironed virtual value of
G^{+}
is
\phi^{+}
.

We next show that replacing
\phi
by
\phi^{+}
leaves the upstream objective unchanged. By the pointwise seller problem,

q(z)=0
and
\pi(z)=0
for every
z\leq 0
.
Therefore, for every
u
,

q(\phi^{+}(u))=q(\phi(u))
and
\pi(\phi^{+}(u))=\pi(\phi(u))
.
Indeed, if
\phi(u)>0
, then
\phi^{+}(u)=\phi(u)
; if
\phi(u)\leq 0
, then both
\phi^{+}(u)
and
\phi(u)
induce zero quality and zero pointwise profit.

It follows immediately that

\Pi(G^{+})=\int_{0}^{1}\pi(\phi^{+}(u))\,du=\int_{0}^{1}\pi(\phi(u))\,du=\Pi(G)
.

For consumer surplus, if
\phi(u)>0
, then monotonicity of
\phi
implies
\phi(t)>0
for every
t\geq u
. Hence
\phi^{+}(t)=\phi(t)
for every
t\geq u
, and therefore

R^{+}(u)=R(u)
and
Q^{+}(u)=Q(u)
.
If
\phi(u)\leq 0
, then

q(\phi^{+}(u))=q(\phi(u))=0
.
Consequently, for every
u\in[0,1]
,

\bigl(Q^{+}(u)-\phi^{+}(u)\bigr)q(\phi^{+}(u))=\bigl(Q(u)-\phi(u)\bigr)q(\phi(u))
.
Thus,
CS(G^{+})=CS(G)
.
Combining the profit and consumer-surplus equalities gives
W_{k}(G^{+})=W_{k}(G)
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1

Profit Biased Upstream Actor

We start by proving the first (straightforward) part of Theorem
1
. To this end, we define the first-best surplus at value
v
:

S(v):=\max_{x\in[0,\bar{Q}]}\{vx-c(x)\}=\pi(v),\qquad v\in[0,1].

Total surplus is given by
\mathrm{TS}=\mathrm{CS}+\Pi
.
Under the seller’s optimal mechanism (with ironed virtual value
\phi
and allocation
q(\phi)
),

\mathrm{TS}=\int_{0}^{1}\Big(Q(u)\,q(\phi(u))-c(q(\phi(u)))\Big)\,du.

Fix any
G
and consider the seller’s optimal mechanism under
G
.
For every realized
v\in[0,1]
and every
q\geq 0
,

vq-c(q)\leq 1\cdot q-c(q)\leq\max_{x\in[0,\bar{Q}]}\{x-c(x)\}=S(1)
.
Hence
\mathrm{TS}\leq S(1)
. Also
\Pi\leq\mathrm{TS}\leq S(1)
.
Using
\mathrm{CS}=\mathrm{TS}-\Pi
,

J_{k}=k\mathrm{CS}+(1-k)\Pi=k\mathrm{TS}+(1-2k)\Pi\leq kS(1)+(1-2k)S(1)=(1-k)S(1).

Now let
G^{*}=\delta_{1}
. Then
v\equiv 1
and the seller chooses
q=\bar{q}=(c^{\prime})^{-1}(1)
and extracts all surplus, so
\mathrm{TS}(G^{*})=\Pi(G^{*})=S(1)
and
\mathrm{CS}(G^{*})=0
.
Thus
J_{k}(G^{*})=(1-k)S(1)
and
G^{*}
is optimal.

For uniqueness, suppose
J_{k}(G)=(1-k)S(1)
.
If
k<1/2
, then the upper bound forces
\mathrm{TS}(G)=S(1)
and
\Pi(G)=S(1)
.
If
k=1/2
, then
J_{1/2}(G)=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathrm{TS}(G)
and equality forces
\mathrm{TS}(G)=S(1)
.
In either case,
\mathrm{TS}(G)=S(1)
implies that the realized per-type surplus equals
S(1)
almost surely.
But for any
v<1
and any
q>0
,
vq-c(q)<1\cdot q-c(q)\leq S(1)
,
so attaining
S(1)
forces
v=1
almost surely, i.e.
G=\delta_{1}
.
∎

Consumer Surplus Biased Upstream Actor

We now proceed to the case where the intermediary attaches weight
k>1/2
on consumer surplus. The first step is to prove that a solution exists.

Proposition 7
.

For each
k\in(1/2,1]
, there exists
\phi^{*}\in\Phi
maximizing
J_{k}
on
\Phi
.

Proof.

We start by proving the following result.

Lemma 3

(Compactness and continuity on
\Phi
)
.

The following hold.

(i)

Every sequence
(\phi_{n})\subset\Phi
has a subsequence converging in

L^{1}([0,1])
to some
\phi\in\Phi
.

(ii)

If
\phi_{n}\to\phi
in
L^{1}([0,1])
with
0\leq\phi_{n},\phi\leq 1
, then

Q_{\phi_{n}}\to Q_{\phi}
in
L^{1}([0,1])
.

(iii)

If
\phi_{n}\to\phi
in
L^{1}([0,1])
with
0\leq\phi_{n},\phi\leq 1
, then

J_{k}(\phi_{n})\to J_{k}(\phi)
.

Proof.

For part (i), let
(\phi_{n})\subset\Phi
. By Helly’s selection theorem, there is a subsequence, still denoted
(\phi_{n})
, and a nondecreasing function
\bar{\phi}:[0,1]\to[0,1]
such that
\phi_{n}(u)\to\bar{\phi}(u)
at every continuity point of
\bar{\phi}
. Define the right-continuous modification

\phi(u):=\lim_{v\downarrow u}\bar{\phi}(v)
for
u\in[0,1)
and
\phi(1):=\lim_{u\uparrow 1}\phi(u)
.
Then
\phi\in\Phi
. Since a monotone function has at most countably many discontinuities,
\phi=\bar{\phi}
at Lebesgue-a.e.
u
. Hence
\phi_{n}(u)\to\phi(u)
for Lebesgue-a.e.
u
. Since
0\leq\phi_{n},\phi\leq 1
, dominated convergence gives

\|\phi_{n}-\phi\|_{L^{1}([0,1])}\to 0
.
This proves sequential compactness of
\Phi
in
L^{1}
.

For part (ii), for every
u<1
,

|Q_{\phi_{n}}(u)-Q_{\phi}(u)|\leq\frac{1}{1-u}\int_{u}^{1}|\phi_{n}(t)-\phi(t)|\,dt.

Integrating over
u
and applying Fubini gives

\|Q_{\phi_{n}}-Q_{\phi}\|_{L^{1}}\leq\int_{0}^{1}w(t)|\phi_{n}(t)-\phi(t)|\,dt,\qquad w(t):=-\ln(1-t)\in L^{1}([0,1]).

Since
|\phi_{n}-\phi|\leq 1
, for every
M>0
,

\int_{0}^{1}w|\phi_{n}-\phi|\leq M\|\phi_{n}-\phi\|_{L^{1}}+\int_{\{w>M\}}w.

First let
n\to\infty
, then let
M\to\infty
. Hence
Q_{\phi_{n}}\to Q_{\phi}
in
L^{1}([0,1])
.

For part (iii), suppose
\phi_{n}\to\phi
in
L^{1}([0,1])
. Note that if f
f:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
is bounded and uniformly continuous, then

f(\phi_{n})\to f(\phi)
in
L^{1}([0,1])
.
Applying this to
f=q
and
f=\pi
, we obtain

q(\phi_{n})\to q(\phi)
and
\pi(\phi_{n})\to\pi(\phi)
in
L^{1}
.
By part (ii),
Q_{\phi_{n}}\to Q_{\phi}
in
L^{1}
. Therefore,

\Pi(\phi_{n})=\int_{0}^{1}\pi(\phi_{n}(u))\,du\longrightarrow\int_{0}^{1}\pi(\phi(u))\,du=\Pi(\phi).

For consumer surplus, write
Q_{n}:=Q_{\phi_{n}}
,
Q:=Q_{\phi}
,
q_{n}:=q(\phi_{n})
, and
q:=q(\phi)
. Since
0\leq Q_{n},\phi_{n},Q,\phi\leq 1
and
0\leq q\leq\bar{q}
,

\displaystyle|CS(\phi_{n})-CS(\phi)|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}|Q_{n}-\phi_{n}|\,|q_{n}-q|\,du+\int_{0}^{1}|(Q_{n}-\phi_{n})-(Q-\phi)|\,q\,du

\displaystyle\leq\|q_{n}-q\|_{1}+\bar{q}\,\|Q_{n}-Q\|_{1}+\bar{q}\,\|\phi_{n}-\phi\|_{1}.

The right-hand side converges to zero. Hence
CS(\phi_{n})\to CS(\phi)
, and therefore
J_{k}(\phi_{n})\to J_{k}(\phi)
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
7

Let
(\phi_{n})\subset\Phi
be a maximizing sequence. By Lemma
3
(i), after passing to a
subsequence,
\phi_{n}\to\phi^{*}
in
L^{1}([0,1])

for some
\phi^{*}\in\Phi
. By Lemma
3
(iii),

J_{k}(\phi_{n})\to J_{k}(\phi^{*})
.
Hence
\phi^{*}
attains the supremum.

∎

Next, for
k\in(1/2,1]
, the upstream actor cares more about consumer surplus than profit, so the degenerate distribution that places all mass at the highest valuation cannot be optimal. Formally,

Lemma 4
.

If
k\in(1/2,1]
, then
\phi\equiv 1
does not maximize
J_{k}
on
\Phi
.

Proof.

Let
\phi\equiv 1
. Then
Q_{\phi}\equiv 1
and
J_{k}(\phi)=(1-k)\pi(1)
.

Fix
k\in(1/2,1]
.
Choose
\delta\in\big(0,\frac{2k-1}{k}\big)
.
Pick
a\in(0,1)
small enough that
11
11

11

This is possible because
(1-a)(-\ln(1-a))/a\to 1
as
a\downarrow 0
.

(1-a)\big(-\ln(1-a)\big)\geq(1-\delta/2)\,a.

(4)

Next, by continuity of
q
at
1
, pick
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
small enough that

q(1-\varepsilon)\geq(1-\delta/2)\,q(1).

(5)

Define

\phi_{\varepsilon}(u)=\begin{cases}1-\varepsilon,&u\in[0,a),\\
1,&u\in[a,1].\end{cases}

Then
\phi_{\varepsilon}\in\Phi
for
u\in[0,a)
,

Q_{\phi_{\varepsilon}}(u)-\phi_{\varepsilon}(u)=\varepsilon\frac{1-a}{1-u},

so
\mathrm{CS}(\phi_{\varepsilon})=\varepsilon(1-a)q(1-\varepsilon)\big(-\ln(1-a)\big)
.
Also,
\Pi(\phi_{\varepsilon})=a\,\pi(1-\varepsilon)+(1-a)\pi(1)
.
Hence,

J_{k}(\phi_{\varepsilon})-J_{k}(\phi)=k\,\varepsilon(1-a)\,q(1-\varepsilon)\,\big(-\ln(1-a)\big)+(1-k)\,a\,(\pi(1-\varepsilon)-\pi(1)).

Since
\pi
is convex and differentiable on
(0,1]
with derivative
\pi^{\prime}(\phi)=q(\phi)
,
we have the standard subgradient inequality at
1
:
\pi(1-\varepsilon)\geq\pi(1)-\varepsilon\,q(1)
,
so
\pi(1-\varepsilon)-\pi(1)\geq-\varepsilon q(1)
. Therefore

J_{k}(\phi_{\varepsilon})-J_{k}(\phi)\geq\varepsilon\Big(k(1-a)q(1-\varepsilon)\big(-\ln(1-a)\big)-(1-k)a\,q(1)\Big).

Using (
4
) and (
5
),

J_{k}(\phi_{\varepsilon})-J_{k}(\phi)\geq\varepsilon\,a\,q(1)\Big(k(1-\delta/2)^{2}-(1-k)\Big).

Since
(1-\delta/2)^{2}\geq 1-\delta
and
\delta<\frac{2k-1}{k}
implies
k(1-\delta)>1-k
, the bracket is strictly positive.
Hence
J_{k}(\phi_{\varepsilon})>J_{k}(\phi)
for these choices, so
\phi\equiv 1
is not optimal.
∎

Next, we establish that in every solution of the problem, every buyer must trade. That is, we must have
\phi^{*}(u)>0
and, thus, there is no exclusion. The reason for this result is that excluding buyers is never optimal, since the upstream actor is unconstrained in the choice of distribution and can add an arbitrarily small amount of trade and generate positive rents without disturbing the rest of the allocation. Formally, we have

Lemma 5
.

Let
k\in(1/2,1]
and let
\phi^{*}
maximize
J_{k}
on
\Phi
. Then
J_{k}(\phi^{*})>0
and

\phi^{*}(u)>0
, for all
u\in(0,1)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
4
, there exists
\hat{\phi}\in\Phi
with
J_{k}(\hat{\phi})>J_{k}(\mathbf{1})\geq 0
, so every maximizer
\phi^{*}
satisfies
J_{k}(\phi^{*})>0
.
Suppose, for contradiction, that
\phi^{*}(u_{0})=0
for some
u_{0}\in(0,1)
. Since
\phi^{*}
is nondecreasing, there exists

a:=\inf\{u\in[0,1]:\phi^{*}(u)>0\}\in(0,1)

such that
\phi^{*}(u)=0
for all
u<a
and
\phi^{*}(u)>0
for all
u>a
. Set
C_{0}:=\int_{a}^{1}\phi^{*}(t)\,dt>0
.
For
\varepsilon>0
, define
b_{\varepsilon}:=\inf\{u\in[0,1]:\phi^{*}(u)\geq\varepsilon\}
and
C_{\varepsilon}:=\int_{b_{\varepsilon}}^{1}\phi^{*}(t)\,dt
.
Then
b_{\varepsilon}\downarrow a
and
C_{\varepsilon}\to C_{0}
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
.

Define the competitor

\phi_{\varepsilon}(u):=\begin{cases}\varepsilon,&u<b_{\varepsilon},\\
\phi^{*}(u),&u\geq b_{\varepsilon}.\end{cases}

Clearly
\phi_{\varepsilon}\in\Phi
. For
u<a
,

Q_{\phi_{\varepsilon}}(u)-\varepsilon=\frac{C_{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon(1-b_{\varepsilon})}{1-u},

so the new contribution on consumer surplus on
[0,a)
equals
kq(\varepsilon)\bigl(C_{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon(1-b_{\varepsilon})\bigr)\,(-\ln(1-a))
.
On
[a,b_{\varepsilon})
we have
\phi^{*}(u)<\varepsilon
, hence

q(\phi^{*}(u))\leq q(\varepsilon)
and
\pi(\phi^{*}(u))\leq\phi^{*}(u)q(\phi^{*}(u))\leq\varepsilon q(\varepsilon)\leq q(\varepsilon)
, and also
0\leq Q_{\phi^{*}}(u)-\phi^{*}(u)\leq 1
. Therefore the old integrand on
[a,b_{\varepsilon})
is bounded by
q(\varepsilon)
, while the two profiles coincide on
[b_{\varepsilon},1]
. Thus

J_{k}(\phi_{\varepsilon})-J_{k}(\phi^{*})\geq q(\varepsilon)\Bigl[k(-\ln(1-a))\bigl(C_{\varepsilon}-\varepsilon(1-b_{\varepsilon})\bigr)-(b_{\varepsilon}-a)\Bigr].

As
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, the bracket converges to

k(-\ln(1-a))C_{0}>0
.

Since
q(\varepsilon)>0
for every
\varepsilon>0
, the right-hand side is strictly positive for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon
, contradicting optimality of
\phi^{*}
. Hence,
\phi^{*}(u)>0
, for all
u\in(0,1)
.
∎

The next result we establish is that the right directional derivative of the objective
J_{k}
exists along feasible directions bounded away from zero. Moreover, at the optimum, a variational inequality must be satisfied.

Lemma 6

(First variation and local variational inequality)
.

Fix
k\in(1/2,1]
.

(i)

Let
\phi\in\Phi
and
\eta\in L^{\infty}([0,1])
. Define
E:=\{u\in[0,1]:\eta(u)\neq 0\}
and let
\phi_{t}:=\phi+t\eta
.
Assume there exist
\delta\in(0,1]
and
\tau>0
such that
\phi(u)\geq\delta
for a.e.
u\in E
and
\phi_{t}\in\Phi
for all
t\in[0,\tau)
.
Then the right derivative exists and

\frac{d}{dt}J_{k}(\phi_{t})\Big|_{t=0+}=\int_{0}^{1}\eta(s)\Big(kA_{\phi}(s)+k(Q_{\phi}(s)-\phi(s))q^{\prime}(\phi(s))+(1-2k)q(\phi(s))\Big)\,ds.

(ii)

Let
\phi^{*}\in\Phi
maximize
J_{k}
over
\Phi
, fix
\phi\in\Phi
, and let

\eta=\phi-\phi^{*},
so that
\phi_{t}=(1-t)\phi^{*}+t\phi
,
t\in[0,1]
. Assume there exists
\delta\in(0,1]
such that
\phi^{*}(u)\geq\delta
for a.e.
u\in E
.
Then

\frac{d}{dt}J_{k}(\phi_{t})\Big|_{t=0+}=\int_{0}^{1}H_{k}[\phi^{*}](u)\,(\phi(u)-\phi^{*}(u))\,du,

and therefore it must be the case that

\int_{0}^{1}H_{k}[\phi^{*}](u)\,(\phi(u)-\phi^{*}(u))\,du\leq 0.

Proof.

Let
E:=\{u:\eta(u)\neq 0\}
and
h(u):=\frac{1}{1-u}\int_{u}^{1}\eta(s)\,ds
.
Then
|h(u)|\leq\|\eta\|_{\infty}
for all
u<1
, and
Q_{\phi+t\eta}=Q_{\phi}+th
.

For part (i), by assumption there exist
\delta>0
and
\tau>0
such that
\phi\geq\delta
a.e. on
E
and
\phi+t\eta\in\Phi
for all
t\in[0,\tau]
. After possibly shrinking
\tau
, we have
\phi+t\eta\in[\delta/2,1]
a.e. on
E
, for all
t\in[0,\tau]
.
Hence on
E
, both
q
and
\pi
are
C^{1}
with bounded derivatives, and
\pi^{\prime}(\phi)=q(\phi)
. Therefore, in
L^{1}(E)
,

\frac{q(\phi+t\eta)-q(\phi)}{t}\to\eta q^{\prime}(\phi),\qquad\frac{\pi(\phi+t\eta)-\pi(\phi)}{t}\to\eta q(\phi),

while on
E^{c}
both differences vanish identically.

Writing
Q_{t}:=Q_{\phi+t\eta}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{J_{k}(\phi+t\eta)-J_{k}(\phi)}{t}=k\int_{0}^{1}h(u)q(\phi+t\eta(u))\,du+k\int_{0}^{1}(Q_{\phi}(u)-\phi(u))\frac{q(\phi+t\eta(u))-q(\phi(u))}{t}\,du-

\displaystyle-k\int_{0}^{1}\eta(u)q(\phi+t\eta(u))\,du+(1-k)\int_{0}^{1}\frac{\pi(\phi+t\eta(u))-\pi(\phi(u))}{t}\,du.

Dominated convergence gives

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}J_{k}(\phi+t\eta)\Big|_{t=0+}=k\int_{0}^{1}h(u)q(\phi(u))\,du+k\int_{0}^{1}(Q_{\phi}(u)-\phi(u))\eta(u)q^{\prime}(\phi(u))\,du

\displaystyle+(1-2k)\int_{0}^{1}\eta(u)q(\phi(u))\,du.

Finally,

\int_{0}^{1}h(u)q(\phi(u))\,du=\int_{0}^{1}\eta(s)\Bigl(\int_{0}^{s}\frac{q(\phi(u))}{1-u}\,du\Bigr)ds=\int_{0}^{1}\eta(s)A_{\phi}(s)\,ds

by Fubini. This proves part (i).

For part (ii), let
\phi_{t}:=(1-t)\phi^{*}+t\phi
and
\eta:=\phi-\phi^{*}
.
Since
\Phi
is convex,
\phi_{t}\in\Phi
for all
t\in[0,1]
. By assumption,
\phi^{*}\geq\delta
a.e. on the support of
\eta
, so part (i) applies and yields

\frac{d}{dt}J_{k}(\phi_{t})\Big|_{t=0+}=\int_{0}^{1}H_{k}[\phi^{*}](u)\,(\phi(u)-\phi^{*}(u))\,du.

Because
\phi^{*}
maximizes
J_{k}
over
\Phi
, the map
t\mapsto J_{k}(\phi_{t})
has a maximum at
t=0
, so its right derivative is nonpositive. Hence

\int_{0}^{1}H_{k}[\phi^{*}](u)\,(\phi(u)-\phi^{*}(u))\,du\leq 0.

∎

We are now ready to prove that the optimal virtual value schedule must hit its upper bound on the terminal interval
[b,1]
for some
b>0
. Formally,

Proposition 8
.

Let
k\in(1/2,1]
and let
\phi^{*}
maximize
J_{k}
on
\Phi
. Then there exists
b\in(0,1)
such that
\phi^{*}(u)=1
for all
u\in[b,1]
.
Consequently,
Q_{\phi^{*}}(u)=1
for all
u\in[b,1)
, so the induced optimal distribution has an atom at
v=1
of size
1-b
.

Proof.

Pick
u_{0}\in(0,1)
and set
\delta:=\phi^{*}(u_{0})>0
(Lemma
5
).
Then for all
u\in[u_{0},1)
,
\phi^{*}(u)\geq\delta
and
q(\phi^{*}(u))\geq q(\delta)>0
.
Hence for
s\in(u_{0},1)
,

A_{\phi^{*}}(s)=\int_{0}^{s}\frac{q(\phi^{*}(u))}{1-u}\,du\geq q(\delta)\int_{u_{0}}^{s}\frac{1}{1-u}\,du=q(\delta)\ln\frac{1-u_{0}}{1-s}\xrightarrow[s\uparrow 1]{}+\infty.

On
[u_{0},1)
, the remaining terms in
H_{k}[\phi^{*}]
are bounded because
Q_{\phi^{*}},\phi^{*}\in[0,1]
,

q(\phi^{*})\leq\bar{q}
, and
q^{\prime}(\phi^{*})
is bounded on
[\delta,1]
(continuity and positivity of
c^{\prime\prime}
on the compact range
[q(\delta),q(1)]
).
Therefore
H_{k}[\phi^{*}](s)\to+\infty
as
s\uparrow 1
, so choose
t\in(u_{0},1)
such that
H_{k}[\phi^{*}](s)>0
for a.e.
s\in(t,1)
.
Assume by contradiction that
\phi^{*}(u)<1
on a set of positive measure in
(t,1)
.
Let
\eta(u):=(1-\phi^{*}(u))\mathbf{1}_{[t,1)}(u)\geq 0
and define
\phi_{\tau}:=\phi^{*}+\tau\eta
for
\tau\in[0,1]
.
Then
\phi_{\tau}\in\Phi
for all
\tau
(convex combination with the constant
1
on
(t,1)
), and for small
\tau
the support of
\eta
lies in
a region where
\phi^{*}\in[\delta,1)
, so Lemma
6
applies and yields

\frac{d}{d\tau}J_{k}(\phi_{\tau})\Big|_{\tau=0}=\int_{t}^{1}\eta(u)\,H_{k}[\phi^{*}](u)\,du>0,

a contradiction to optimality of
\phi^{*}
.
Hence
\phi^{*}(u)=1
a.e. on
(t,1)
; right-continuity implies
\phi^{*}(u)=1
for all
u\in[b,1]
for some
b<1
.
Finally, since the degenerate distribution
G_{\delta_{1}}
cannot be optimal, we get
b>0
.
The atom statement follows because if
u\in[b,1)
then
Q_{\phi^{*}}(u)=\frac{1}{1-u}\int_{u}^{1}1\,dt=1
.
∎

Next, we define the (unique) cutoff

b:=\inf\{u\in[0,1]:\phi^{*}(t)=1\ \forall t\in[u,1]\}\in(0,1).

We now argue that on
[0,b)
, no interval can have a constant virtual value as a solution to the upstream actor’s problem. We start by proving the derivative of
H_{k}(\phi^{*})
is strictly positive on a flat interior interval. We let
h:=H_{k}(\phi^{*})
to save on notation.

Lemma 7
.

Let
(\ell,r)\subset(0,1)
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
satisfy
\phi^{*}(u)=\gamma
for all
u\in(\ell,r)
.
Then
h=H_{k}[\phi^{*}]
is absolutely continuous on
(\ell,r)
and satisfies

h^{\prime}(u)\ \geq\ k\,\frac{q(\gamma)}{1-u}\ >\ 0\qquad\text{for a.e.\ }u\in(\ell,r).

In particular,
h
is strictly increasing on
(\ell,r)
.

Proof.

On
(\ell,r)
,
\phi^{*}\equiv\gamma\in(0,1)
, so
q(\phi^{*})\equiv q(\gamma)>0
and
q^{\prime}(\phi^{*})\equiv q^{\prime}(\gamma)\in(0,\infty)

are constants. Also
Q_{\phi^{*}}
is absolutely continuous and satisfies for a.e.
u
,

(Q_{\phi^{*}})^{\prime}(u)=\frac{Q_{\phi^{*}}(u)-\phi^{*}(u)}{1-u}=\frac{Q_{\phi^{*}}(u)-\gamma}{1-u}\ \geq\ 0,

because
Q_{\phi^{*}}(u)=\frac{1}{1-u}\int_{u}^{1}\phi^{*}(t)\,dt\geq\gamma
by monotonicity of
\phi^{*}
.

Moreover,
A_{\phi^{*}}
is absolutely continuous with

A_{\phi^{*}}^{\prime}(u)=\frac{q(\phi^{*}(u))}{1-u}=\frac{q(\gamma)}{1-u}\qquad\text{for a.e.\ }u\in(\ell,r).

Since
(1-2k)q(\gamma)
is constant on
(\ell,r)
, differentiating
h=H_{k}[\phi^{*}]
a.e. on
(\ell,r)
yields

h^{\prime}(u)=k\,A_{\phi^{*}}^{\prime}(u)+k\,q^{\prime}(\gamma)\,(Q_{\phi^{*}})^{\prime}(u)\ \geq\ k\,\frac{q(\gamma)}{1-u}\ >\ 0\qquad\text{a.e. on }(\ell,r).

Thus
h
is absolutely continuous with strictly positive derivative a.e., hence strictly increasing.
∎

Equipped with this result, we are ready to prove that the solution to the upstream actor’s problem cannot feature bunching.

Proposition 9
.

Fix
k\in(1/2,1]
and let
\phi^{*}
be any maximizer of
J_{k}
on
\Phi
. Let
b
be its cutoff. Then, there cannot be an interval contained in
(0,b)
where
\phi^{*}
is constant. Equivalently, there is no bunching on the interior trading block.

Proof.

Suppose, for contradiction, that there exist
\gamma\in(0,1)
and a maximal open interval

(\ell,r)\subset[0,b)
such that

\phi^{*}(u)=\gamma
for all
u\in(\ell,r)
.
Let
h
denote the representative of
H_{k}[\phi^{*}]
from Lemma
7
. Since
h^{\prime}(u)>0
for a.e.
u\in(\ell,r)
, the function
h
is strictly increasing on
(\ell,r)
.

Set

m:=\frac{\ell+r}{2},\qquad D:=\int_{m}^{r}h(u)\,du-\int_{\ell}^{m}h(u)\,du.

Because
h
is strictly increasing on
(\ell,r)
, we have
D>0
.

Fix
0<\varepsilon<\min\{\gamma/2,\;1-\gamma\}
, and define

\phi_{\varepsilon}(u):=\begin{cases}\min\{\phi^{*}(u),\gamma-\varepsilon\},&u<m,\\
\max\{\phi^{*}(u),\gamma+\varepsilon\},&u\geq m.\end{cases}

Then
\phi_{\varepsilon}\in\Phi
.

We first dispose of the case
\ell=0
, in which case we let
m:=r/2
. Now,
\phi_{\varepsilon}-\phi^{*}
is supported on a set on which
\phi^{*}\geq\gamma/2
, for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon
. Hence the variational inequality from Lemma
6
applies. Let
h:=H_{k}[\phi^{*}]
. On
(0,r)
, Lemma
7
implies that
h
is strictly increasing. Therefore

\int_{0}^{r}h(u)(\phi_{\varepsilon}(u)-\phi^{*}(u))\,du=\varepsilon\left(\int_{m}^{r}h(u)\,du-\int_{0}^{m}h(u)\,du\right)>0.

Outside
(0,r)
, the perturbation is nonzero only on

E_{\varepsilon}:=\{u\geq r:\phi^{*}(u)<\gamma+\varepsilon\}
.
Since
(0,r)
is a maximal flat interval at level
\gamma
, monotonicity of
\phi^{*}
implies

\lambda(E_{\varepsilon})\to 0
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
. Also
h
is bounded on
E_{\varepsilon}
for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon
. Hence

\int_{E_{\varepsilon}}h(u)(\phi_{\varepsilon}(u)-\phi^{*}(u))\,du=o(\varepsilon)
. Thus, for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon
,

\int_{0}^{1}h(u)(\phi_{\varepsilon}(u)-\phi^{*}(u))\,du>0

contradicting the variational inequality. Hence
\ell>0
.

Now define

a_{\varepsilon}:=\sup\bigl(\{u\leq\ell:\phi^{*}(u)\leq\gamma-\varepsilon\}\cup\{0\}\bigr),\qquad b_{\varepsilon}:=\inf\bigl(\{u\geq r:\phi^{*}(u)\geq\gamma+\varepsilon\}\cup\{1\}\bigr),

and
E_{\varepsilon}:=(a_{\varepsilon},\ell)\cup(r,b_{\varepsilon})
.
By monotonicity of
\phi^{*}
and maximality of
(\ell,r)
,
a_{\varepsilon}\uparrow\ell
,
b_{\varepsilon}\downarrow r
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
.

On the flat block we have

\phi_{\varepsilon}(u)-\phi^{*}(u)=\begin{cases}-\varepsilon,&u\in(\ell,m),\\
+\varepsilon,&u\in[m,r),\end{cases}

hence

\int_{\ell}^{r}h(u)\bigl(\phi_{\varepsilon}(u)-\phi^{*}(u)\bigr)\,du=-\varepsilon\int_{\ell}^{m}h(u)\,du+\varepsilon\int_{m}^{r}h(u)\,du=\varepsilon D.

Also, whenever
\phi_{\varepsilon}(u)\neq\phi^{*}(u)
, we necessarily have
\phi^{*}(u)\in(\gamma-\varepsilon,\gamma+\varepsilon)\subset(\gamma/2,1]
.
Since
\varepsilon<\gamma/2
, Lemma
6
(ii) applies to the competitor
\phi=\phi_{\varepsilon}
with

\delta=\gamma/2
, and yields

0\geq\int_{0}^{1}h(u)\bigl(\phi_{\varepsilon}(u)-\phi^{*}(u)\bigr)\,du.

It remains to estimate the contribution on
E_{\varepsilon}
. Choose
\rho>0
such that
[\ell-\rho,r+\rho]\subset(0,1)
.
Since
a_{\varepsilon}\uparrow\ell
and
b_{\varepsilon}\downarrow r
, for all sufficiently small

\varepsilon
we have
E_{\varepsilon}\subset(\ell-\rho,r+\rho)
. Fix such an
\varepsilon
. For every
u\in E_{\varepsilon}
we have
\phi^{*}(u)\in(\gamma-\varepsilon,\gamma+\varepsilon)\subset[\gamma/2,1]
. Define

M_{0}:=q(1)\int_{0}^{r+\rho}\frac{ds}{1-s}<\infty,\qquad M_{1}:=\sup_{z\in[\gamma/2,1]}|q^{\prime}(z)|<\infty.

Then for every
u\in E_{\varepsilon}
,

A_{\phi^{*}}(u)=\int_{0}^{u}\frac{q(\phi^{*}(s))}{1-s}\,ds\leq q(1)\int_{0}^{r+\rho}\frac{ds}{1-s}=M_{0}.

Also
0\leq Q_{\phi^{*}}(u)\leq 1
,
0\leq\phi^{*}(u)\leq 1
. Since
\phi^{*}(u)>\gamma/2>0
on
E_{\varepsilon}
, the explicit formula for
h
gives, for a.e.
u\in E_{\varepsilon}
,

|h(u)|\leq kM_{0}+kM_{1}+|1-2k|q(1)=:M
.
Thus,
M<\infty
is independent of
\varepsilon
for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon
.

Using
|\phi_{\varepsilon}-\phi^{*}|\leq\varepsilon
on
E_{\varepsilon}
, we obtain

\left|\int_{E_{\varepsilon}}h(u)\bigl(\phi_{\varepsilon}(u)-\phi^{*}(u)\bigr)\,du\right|\leq\varepsilon\int_{E_{\varepsilon}}|h(u)|\,du\leq\varepsilon M|E_{\varepsilon}|.

But
|E_{\varepsilon}|=(\ell-a_{\varepsilon})+(b_{\varepsilon}-r)\to 0
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, so

\int_{E_{\varepsilon}}h(u)\bigl(\phi_{\varepsilon}(u)-\phi^{*}(u)\bigr)\,du=o(\varepsilon).

Combining the block term and the remainder term,

\int_{0}^{1}h(u)\bigl(\phi_{\varepsilon}(u)-\phi^{*}(u)\bigr)\,du=\varepsilon D+o(\varepsilon).

Since
D>0
, the right-hand side is strictly positive for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
,
contradicting

0\geq\int_{0}^{1}h(u)\bigl(\phi_{\varepsilon}(u)-\phi^{*}(u)\bigr)\,du.

The contradiction proves that no such interval
(\ell,r)
can exist.
∎

An immediate corollary of our results so far is that the optimal
\phi^{*}
must be strictly increasing on
(0,b)
.

Corollary 7

(Strict increase and interior region)
.

Let
k\in(1/2,1]
and
\phi^{*}
be any maximizer of
J_{k}
on
\Phi
. Let
b
be its cutoff.
Then
\phi^{*}
is strictly increasing on
(0,b)
and satisfies
0<\phi^{*}(u)<1
for all
u\in(0,b)
and
\phi^{*}(u)=1
for all
u\in[b,1]
.

Proof.

Lemma
5
gives
\phi^{*}>0
on
(0,1)
and Proposition
8
gives
\phi^{*}(u)=1
on
[b,1]
and
\phi^{*}(u)<1
for
u<b
.
If
\phi^{*}
were not strictly increasing on
(0,b)
, then there exist
x<y
in
(0,b)
with
\phi^{*}(x)=\phi^{*}(y)=:\gamma
.
Monotonicity implies
\phi^{*}(u)=\gamma
for all
u\in(x,y)
, and Lemma
5
and
y<b
imply
\gamma\in(0,1)
.
By Lemma
7
, the corresponding VI density
h=H_{k}[\phi^{*}]
has
h^{\prime}(u)>0
a.e. on
(x,y)
,
so Proposition
9
rules out such a flat block. Hence
\phi^{*}
is strictly increasing on
(0,b)
.
∎

Up until now we have proved part 1 and part 2(i) of Theorem
1
. We now proceed to part 2(ii). The next Proposition establishes that once we know the optimizer has no bunching on the interior region
(so
\phi^{*}
is strictly increasing there), the monotonicity constraint is locally slack enough to force the pointwise first-variation density to vanish a.e. on the interior block.

Proposition 10
.

Fix
k\in(1/2,1]
and let
\phi^{*}\in\Phi
maximize
J_{k}
over
\Phi
.
Let
b\in(0,1)
be its cutoff, so that
\phi^{*}(u)=1
for all
u\in[b,1]
.
Assume moreover (by Corollary
7
) that
\phi^{*}
is strictly increasing on
(0,b)
and
satisfies
0<\phi^{*}(u)<1
for all
u\in(0,b)
.
Let
H_{k}[\phi^{*}](u)

be the first-variation density defined in (
3
).
Then
H_{k}[\phi^{*}](u)=0
for a.e.
u\in(0,b)
.

Proof.

Fix
t\in(0,b)
. By Corollary
7
,
\phi^{*}(t)\in(0,1)
.
Choose
\varepsilon_{0}\in(0,1)
such that

0<\varepsilon_{0}<\phi^{*}(t)/2
and
\varepsilon_{0}<1-\phi^{*}(t)
.
Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
throughout, and set
\underline{t}:=t/2\in(0,t)
.

Since
b<1
, we have
1-u\geq 1-b>0
for all
u\in[0,b]
.
On
[0,b]
we also have
0\leq q(\phi^{*}(u))\leq q(1)<\infty
.
Hence, for
u\in[0,b]
,

A_{\phi^{*}}(u)=\int_{0}^{u}\frac{q(\phi^{*}(s))}{1-s}\,ds\leq\frac{1}{1-b}\int_{0}^{b}q(\phi^{*}(s))\,ds\leq\frac{b\,q(1)}{1-b}<\infty.

Moreover, on
[\underline{t},b]
we have
\phi^{*}(u)\geq\phi^{*}(\underline{t})>0
, so
q^{\prime}(\phi^{*}(u))

is bounded on
[\underline{t},b]
(since
q^{\prime}
is continuous on any compact subset of
(0,1]
).
Since also
0\leq Q_{\phi^{*}},\phi^{*}\leq 1
on
[0,1]
, each term in
h=H_{k}[\phi^{*}]
(cf. (
3
))
is bounded on
[\underline{t},b]
. In particular, since
h\in L^{1}([\underline{t},b])
it follows that
h\in L^{1}([t,b])
and
h\in L^{1}([\underline{t},t])
.

We first prove

\int_{t}^{b}h(u)\,du\leq 0
.
Fix
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
and define

\phi_{\varepsilon}^{+}(u):=\begin{cases}\phi^{*}(u),&u<t,\\
\min\{\phi^{*}(u)+\varepsilon,1\},&u\geq t.\end{cases}

Then
\phi_{\varepsilon}^{+}\in\Phi
.

Define
\tau_{\varepsilon}:=\inf\{u\in[t,b]:\phi^{*}(u)\geq 1-\varepsilon\}\in[t,b]
.
This is well-defined because
\phi^{*}(b)=1
. By right-continuity of
\phi^{*}
,
\phi^{*}(\tau_{\varepsilon})\geq 1-\varepsilon
,
and since
\phi^{*}(u)<1
for every
u<b
, we have
\tau_{\varepsilon}\uparrow b
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
.

By construction,

\phi_{\varepsilon}^{+}(u)-\phi^{*}(u)=\begin{cases}0,&u<t,\\
\varepsilon,&t\leq u<\tau_{\varepsilon},\\
1-\phi^{*}(u)\in[0,\varepsilon],&\tau_{\varepsilon}\leq u<b,\\
0,&u\geq b.\end{cases}

Since
\phi^{*}(u)\geq\phi^{*}(t)>0
for all
u\geq t
, Lemma
6
(ii) applies to the competitor

\phi=\phi_{\varepsilon}^{+}
with
\delta:=\phi^{*}(t)/2
. Hence

0\geq\int_{0}^{1}h(u)\bigl(\phi_{\varepsilon}^{+}(u)-\phi^{*}(u)\bigr)\,du=\varepsilon\int_{t}^{\tau_{\varepsilon}}h(u)\,du+\int_{\tau_{\varepsilon}}^{b}h(u)\bigl(1-\phi^{*}(u)\bigr)\,du.

Using
0\leq 1-\phi^{*}(u)\leq\varepsilon
on
[\tau_{\varepsilon},b)
, we get

0\geq\varepsilon\int_{t}^{\tau_{\varepsilon}}h(u)\,du-\varepsilon\int_{\tau_{\varepsilon}}^{b}|h(u)|\,du.

Divide by
\varepsilon>0
:

\int_{t}^{\tau_{\varepsilon}}h(u)\,du\leq\int_{\tau_{\varepsilon}}^{b}|h(u)|\,du.

Letting
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
, and using
h\in L^{1}([t,b])
together with

\tau_{\varepsilon}\uparrow b
, yields

\int_{t}^{b}h(u)\,du\leq 0
.

We now prove
\int_{t}^{b}h(u)\,du\geq 0
.
Fix again
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
and define

\phi_{\varepsilon}^{-}(u):=\begin{cases}\min\{\phi^{*}(u),\phi^{*}(t)-\varepsilon\},&u<t,\\
\phi^{*}(u)-\varepsilon,&t\leq u<b,\\
\phi^{*}(u),&u\geq b.\end{cases}

Then
\phi_{\varepsilon}^{-}\in\Phi
and is
[0,1]
-valued because
\varepsilon<\phi^{*}(t)
.

Let
L_{\varepsilon}:=\{u\in[0,t):\phi^{*}(u)>\phi^{*}(t)-\varepsilon\}
.
On
L_{\varepsilon}
we have

\phi_{\varepsilon}^{-}(u)-\phi^{*}(u)=\phi^{*}(t)-\varepsilon-\phi^{*}(u)\in[-\varepsilon,0]
,
while on
[t,b)
we have
\phi_{\varepsilon}^{-}(u)-\phi^{*}(u)=-\varepsilon
, and outside
L_{\varepsilon}\cup[t,b)
the difference is zero.

Since
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}<\phi^{*}(t)/2
, on the support of

\phi_{\varepsilon}^{-}-\phi^{*}
we have

\phi^{*}(u)\geq\phi^{*}(t)-\varepsilon\geq\phi^{*}(t)/2>0
.
Thus Lemma
6
(ii) applies to the competitor
\phi=\phi_{\varepsilon}^{-}
with

\delta:=\phi^{*}(t)/2
, giving

0\geq\int_{0}^{1}h(u)\bigl(\phi_{\varepsilon}^{-}(u)-\phi^{*}(u)\bigr)\,du=-\varepsilon\int_{t}^{b}h(u)\,du+\int_{L_{\varepsilon}}h(u)\bigl(\phi^{*}(t)-\varepsilon-\phi^{*}(u)\bigr)\,du.

Hence

\int_{t}^{b}h(u)\,du\geq\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{L_{\varepsilon}}h(u)\bigl(\phi^{*}(t)-\varepsilon-\phi^{*}(u)\bigr)\,du.

Since

\bigl|\phi^{*}(t)-\varepsilon-\phi^{*}(u)\bigr|\leq\varepsilon
for all
u\in L_{\varepsilon}
, we obtain

\left|\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int_{L_{\varepsilon}}h(u)\bigl(\phi^{*}(t)-\varepsilon-\phi^{*}(u)\bigr)\,du\right|\leq\int_{L_{\varepsilon}}|h(u)|\,du.

Now define

a_{\varepsilon}:=\sup\bigl(\{u\leq t:\phi^{*}(u)\leq\phi^{*}(t)-\varepsilon\}\cup\{0\}\bigr)\leq t.

By monotonicity,
L_{\varepsilon}\subset(a_{\varepsilon},t)
.
By strict increase of
\phi^{*}
on
(0,b)
, we have
a_{\varepsilon}\uparrow t
as
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
.
Indeed, if
\delta\in(0,t)
, then
\phi^{*}(t-\delta)<\phi^{*}(t)
; hence for every
0<\varepsilon<\phi^{*}(t)-\phi^{*}(t-\delta)

we have
t-\delta\in\{u\leq t:\phi^{*}(u)\leq\phi^{*}(t)-\varepsilon\}
, so
a_{\varepsilon}\geq t-\delta
.

Therefore, for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon
,
a_{\varepsilon}\geq\underline{t}
and
L_{\varepsilon}\subset(a_{\varepsilon},t)\subset[\underline{t},t]
. Since
h\in L^{1}([\underline{t},t])
,

\int_{L_{\varepsilon}}|h(u)|\,du\leq\int_{a_{\varepsilon}}^{t}|h(u)|\,du\longrightarrow 0\qquad\text{as }\varepsilon\downarrow 0.

Letting
\varepsilon\downarrow 0
yields

\int_{t}^{b}h(u)\,du\geq 0
.

Combining the two inequalities, we obtain
\int_{t}^{b}h(u)\,du=0
for all
t\in(0,b)
.

Now fix any
s\in(0,b)
. Since
\phi^{*}(s)>0
and
\phi^{*}
is nondecreasing, the same boundedness
argument as above gives
h\in L^{1}([s,b])
.
Define

F_{s}(t):=\int_{t}^{b}h(u)\,du,\qquad t\in[s,b].

Then
F_{s}
is absolutely continuous on
[s,b]
and satisfies
F_{s}^{\prime}(t)=-h(t)
for a.e.
t\in(s,b)
. But
F_{s}(t)\equiv 0
on
[s,b]
, so
h(t)=0
for a.e.
t\in(s,b)
. Finally, take any sequence
s_{n}\downarrow 0
(for instance
s_{n}=b/n
). Since
(0,b)=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}(s_{n},b)
, it follows that
h(u)=0
for a.e.
u\in(0,b)
.
∎

In order to reduce the Euler-Lagrange-type equation of Proposition
10
and prove the uniqueness of the solution to the upstream actor’s problem, in addition to Assumption
1
, from now on, we also maintain Assumption
2
.

Let
\lambda:=(2k-1)/k\in(0,1]
. First, under Assumption
2
, we can prove that
\phi^{*}(0)>0.

Lemma 8
.

Fix
k\in(1/2,1]
and let
\phi^{*}\in\Phi
maximize
J_{k}
over
\Phi
. Let
b\in(0,1)
be the cutoff, so that
\phi^{*}(u)=1
for all
u\in[b,1]
. Then
\phi^{*}(0)>0
.

Proof.

We first show the following result.

Lemma 9
.

Maintain Assumption
2
. Then
\lim_{q\downarrow 0}q\,c^{\prime\prime}(q)=0
.
Equivalently,

\lim_{\phi\downarrow 0}\frac{q(\phi)}{dq/d\phi(\phi)}=0.

Proof.

Set
\beta:=(3k-1)/(2k-1)>0
. By Assumption
2
,

-\,q\,c^{\prime\prime\prime}(q)/c^{\prime\prime}(q)<\beta
for all
q\in(0,\bar{q}]
. Equivalently,

\frac{d}{dq}\log c^{\prime\prime}(q)>-\beta/q
.
Fix
q\in(0,\bar{q}/2]
and
s\in[q,2q]
. Integrating from
q
to
s
gives
\log c^{\prime\prime}(s)-\log c^{\prime\prime}(q)>-\beta\log(s/q)
,
hence
c^{\prime\prime}(s)\geq c^{\prime\prime}(q)\Big(q/s\Big)^{\beta}\geq 2^{-\beta}c^{\prime\prime}(q)
.
Therefore,

c^{\prime}(2q)-c^{\prime}(q)=\int_{q}^{2q}c^{\prime\prime}(s)\,ds\geq q\,2^{-\beta}c^{\prime\prime}(q).

Since
c^{\prime}(0)=0
and
c^{\prime}
is continuous at
0
, the left-hand side tends to
0
as
q\downarrow 0
. Hence
q\,c^{\prime\prime}(q)\to 0
.

Finally, because
q(\phi)\downarrow 0
as
\phi\downarrow 0
and
\frac{dq}{d\phi}(\phi)=1/c^{\prime\prime}(q(\phi))
, we get

\frac{q(\phi)}{dq/d\phi(\phi)}=q(\phi)c^{\prime\prime}(q(\phi))\to 0.

∎

Proof of Lemma
8

Suppose, for contradiction, that
\phi^{*}(0)=0
.
Since
\phi^{*}\in\Phi
is right-continuous and nondecreasing,
\phi^{*}(u)\downarrow 0
as
u\downarrow 0
.

For every
u\in[0,b)
,

Q(u)=\frac{1}{1-u}\int_{u}^{1}\phi^{*}(t)\,dt\geq\frac{1}{1-u}\int_{b}^{1}1\,dt=\frac{1-b}{1-u}\geq 1-b.

Hence there exists
\varepsilon_{1}\in(0,b)
such that
\phi^{*}(u)\leq(1-b)/2
for all
u\in[0,\varepsilon_{1}]
.
Therefore,
Q(u)-\phi^{*}(u)\geq(1-b)/2
for all
u\in[0,\varepsilon_{1}]
.

By the auxiliary lemma,

\frac{q(z)}{dq/d\phi(z)}\to 0\qquad\text{as }z\downarrow 0.

Choose
\delta>0
such that

(2k-1)q(z)\leq\frac{k(1-b)}{4}\frac{dq}{d\phi}(z)\qquad\forall z\in(0,\delta].

Since
\phi^{*}(u)\downarrow 0
as
u\downarrow 0
, there exists
\varepsilon_{2}\in(0,\varepsilon_{1}]
such that
\phi^{*}(u)\leq\delta
for all
u\in[0,\varepsilon_{2}]
.

For every
u\in(0,\varepsilon_{2})
, we have
A_{\phi^{*}}(u)\geq 0
, so

H_{k}[\phi^{*}](u)=kA_{\phi^{*}}(u)+k(Q(u)-\phi^{*}(u))\frac{dq}{d\phi}(\phi^{*}(u))+(1-2k)q(\phi^{*}(u))

\geq k\frac{1-b}{2}\frac{dq}{d\phi}(\phi^{*}(u))-(2k-1)q(\phi^{*}(u))\geq k\frac{1-b}{4}\frac{dq}{d\phi}(\phi^{*}(u))>0.

This contradicts Proposition
10
, which states that
H_{k}[\phi^{*}](u)=0
for a.e.
u\in(0,b)
. Therefore
\phi^{*}(0)>0
.
∎

Next, recall the notation we use:

x(u)=q(\phi^{*}(u)),\quad A(u)=\int_{0}^{u}\frac{x(s)}{1-s}\,ds,\quad Q(u)=\frac{1}{1-u}\int_{u}^{1}\phi^{*}(t)\,dt,\quad u\in[0,b].

(6)

Since
0\leq x\leq q(1)=\bar{q}
, the function
A
is continuous on
[0,b]
.
Since
0\leq\phi^{*}\leq 1
, the function
Q
is continuous on
[0,b]
.

By Proposition
10
,
\phi^{*}
satisfies the Euler-Lagrange equality

kA(u)+k(Q(u)-\phi^{*}(u))q^{\prime}(\phi^{*}(u))+(1-2k)q(\phi^{*}(u))=0\qquad\text{for a.e. }u\in(0,b).

(7)

Because
\phi^{*}(u)>0
on
(0,b)
, we obtain

x(u)>0,\qquad\phi^{*}(u)=c^{\prime}(x(u)),\qquad q^{\prime}(\phi^{*}(u))=\frac{1}{c^{\prime\prime}(x(u))}\qquad\text{for every }u\in(0,b).

Hence (
7
) is equivalent to

A(u)+\frac{Q(u)-c^{\prime}(x(u))}{c^{\prime\prime}(x(u))}-\lambda x(u)=0\qquad\text{for a.e. }u\in(0,b),

(8)

or, equivalently,

Q(u)=c^{\prime}(x(u))+c^{\prime\prime}(x(u))(\lambda x(u)-A(u))\qquad\text{for a.e. }u\in(0,b).

(9)

Since
Q(u)\geq\phi^{*}(u)=c^{\prime}(x(u))
for every
u\in(0,b)
, (
9
) implies

\lambda x(u)-A(u)\geq 0
for a.e.
u\in(0,b)

Define the auxiliary function

H(A,z):=c^{\prime}(z)+c^{\prime\prime}(z)(\lambda z-A),\qquad(A,z)\in\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty).

(10)

Equation
9
above says that the current induced value
Q(u)
must equal the current marginal cost plus a wedge determined by the upstream actor’s current desire to sustain rents. Since
Q(u)\geq c^{\prime}(x(u))
, the upstream actor chooses an interior branch with
\lambda x(u)-A(u)\geq 0
. We start by showing that under Assumption
2
, the map
z\longmapsto H(A,z)

is strictly increasing.

Lemma 10
.

Fix
k>1/2
, so
\lambda>0
and maintain Assumption
2
. Let
I\subset(0,\bar{q}]
be an interval and let
A\geq 0
satisfy

A\leq\lambda z
for all
z\in I.

Then the map
z\longmapsto H(A,z)

is strictly increasing on
I
. Equivalently,

\partial_{z}H(A,z)>0
for all
z\in I
.

Proof.

Fix
\lambda>0
,
A\geq 0
, and
z\in I
. We have

\partial_{z}H(A,z)=(1+\lambda)c^{\prime\prime}(z)+(\lambda z-A)c^{\prime\prime\prime}(z).

If
c^{\prime\prime\prime}(z)\geq 0
, then

\partial_{z}H(A,z)\geq(1+\lambda)c^{\prime\prime}(z)>0
.

If
c^{\prime\prime\prime}(z)<0
, then
A\leq\lambda z
implies

(\lambda z-A)c^{\prime\prime\prime}(z)\geq\lambda z\,c^{\prime\prime\prime}(z)
,
hence

\partial_{z}H(A,z)\geq(1+\lambda)c^{\prime\prime}(z)+\lambda z\,c^{\prime\prime\prime}(z)=c^{\prime\prime}(z)\left(1+\lambda-\lambda\left(-z\frac{c^{\prime\prime\prime}(z)}{c^{\prime\prime}(z)}\right)\right).

By assumption,
-\,z\,c^{\prime\prime\prime}(z)/c^{\prime\prime}(z)<1+1/\lambda
, so

1+\lambda-\lambda\left(-z\frac{c^{\prime\prime\prime}(z)}{c^{\prime\prime}(z)}\right)>1+\lambda-\lambda\left(1+\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)=1-\frac{\lambda}{\lambda}=0.

The inequality is strict, and
c^{\prime\prime}(z)>0
, so again
\partial_{z}H(A,z)>0
.

Thus
\partial_{z}H(A,z)>0
for every
z\in I
, and so
z\mapsto H(A,z)
is strictly increasing on
I
.
∎

This is a free-boundary problem because the endpoint
b
is not fixed ex ante. The interior branch ends exactly when the optimizer reaches the efficient top tail
\phi^{*}=1
. In Lemma
11
below, we prove that at the cutoff
b
, the optimal
\phi^{*}
does not jump and we identify the terminal conditions that must hold. Moreover, we establish that the function
x=q\circ\phi^{*}
is continuous.

Lemma 11
.

We have
\phi^{*}(b^{-})=1.
Consequently,
x(b^{-})=\bar{q}
and
A(b)=\lambda\bar{q}.
Moreover, the function
x(\cdot)
is continuous on
(0,b)
.

Proof.

Set
p:=\phi^{*}(b^{-})=\lim_{u\uparrow b}\phi^{*}(u)\in(0,1]
. We first prove that
p=1
.

Assume for contradiction that
p<1
.
Choose
\tau>0
so small that
p<1-\tau<1
. Fix any
\delta\in(0,1-b)
and define
\eta_{\delta}(u):=-\mathbf{1}_{[b,b+\delta)}(u)
and
\phi_{t}(u):=\phi^{*}(u)+t\eta_{\delta}(u)
for
t\in[0,\tau]
.
Because
\phi^{*}(u)=1
for every
u\in[b,1]
and
1-\tau>p
, the perturbed profile
\phi_{t}
is still right-continuous, nondecreasing, and
[0,1]
-valued for every
t\in[0,\tau]
. Thus
\phi_{t}\in\Phi
for every
t\in[0,\tau]
.

The support of
\eta_{\delta}
lies in
[b,b+\delta)
, where
\phi^{*}=1
; in particular,
\phi^{*}
is bounded away from
0
on the support of
\eta_{\delta}
.
Therefore the one-sided directional-derivative formula established in Lemma
6
applies and yields

\frac{d}{dt}J_{k}(\phi_{t})\Big|_{t=0^{+}}=\int_{0}^{1}\eta_{\delta}(u)\Big(kA(u)+k(Q(u)-\phi^{*}(u))q^{\prime}(\phi^{*}(u))+(1-2k)q(\phi^{*}(u))\Big)\,du.

Since on
[b,b+\delta)
we have
\phi^{*}(u)=1
and
Q(u)=1
, this becomes

\frac{d}{dt}J_{k}(\phi_{t})\Big|_{t=0^{+}}=-\int_{b}^{b+\delta}\Big(kA(u)+(1-2k)\bar{q}\Big)\,du=-k\int_{b}^{b+\delta}\bigl(A(u)-\lambda\bar{q}\bigr)\,du.

Optimality of
\phi^{*}
implies the one-sided derivative is nonpositive, hence

\int_{b}^{b+\delta}\bigl(A(u)-\lambda\bar{q}\bigr)\,du\geq 0.

Divide by
\delta
and let
\delta\downarrow 0
.
Since
A
is continuous, we obtain

A(b)\geq\lambda\bar{q}.

(11)

We now derive the opposite strict inequality from the interior Euler-Lagrange equation.
Because (
7
) holds on a set of full measure in
(0,b)
, there exists a sequence
u_{n}\uparrow b
such that (
8
) holds at every
u_{n}
.
Evaluating (
8
) at
u_{n}
gives

A(u_{n})+\frac{Q(u_{n})-c^{\prime}(x(u_{n}))}{c^{\prime\prime}(x(u_{n}))}-\lambda x(u_{n})=0.

As
n\to\infty
, monotonicity of
\phi^{*}
yields
\phi^{*}(u_{n})\to p
, hence continuity of
q
gives
x(u_{n})\to q(p)
.
Also
A(u_{n})\to A(b)
and
Q(u_{n})\to 1
by continuity of
A
and
Q
.
Therefore

A(b)+\frac{1-c^{\prime}(q(p))}{c^{\prime\prime}(q(p))}-\lambda q(p)=0.

Since
c^{\prime}(q(p))=p
, this is

A(b)=\lambda q(p)-(1-p)q^{\prime}(p).

(12)

Because
p<1
, we have
q(p)<q(1)=\bar{q}
and
q^{\prime}(p)>0
.
Hence
A(b)=\lambda q(p)-(1-p)q^{\prime}(p)<\lambda q(p)<\lambda\bar{q}
,
contradicting (
11
).
Thus,
p=1
.

Therefore,
x(b^{-})=q(1)=\bar{q}
.
Taking
p=1
in (
12
) gives
A(b)=\lambda q(1)=\lambda\bar{q}
.
For the continuity of
x(\cdot)
, fix
u_{0}\in(0,b)
.
Since
x
is monotone, the one-sided limits

x^{-}:=\lim_{u\uparrow u_{0}}x(u)
and
x^{+}:=\lim_{u\downarrow u_{0}}x(u)

exist and satisfy
0<x^{-}\leq x^{+}<\bar{q}
.
Assume for contradiction that
x^{-}<x^{+}
.

Because (
7
) holds on a set of full measure in
(0,b)
, there exist sequences
u_{n}^{-}\uparrow u_{0}
and
u_{n}^{+}\downarrow u_{0}
such that (
9
) holds at every
u_{n}^{-}
and
u_{n}^{+}
.
Thus,
Q(u_{n}^{-})=H(A(u_{n}^{-}),x(u_{n}^{-}))
and
Q(u_{n}^{+})=H(A(u_{n}^{+}),x(u_{n}^{+}))
.
Passing to the limit and using continuity of
A
and
Q
, we obtain

Q(u_{0})=H(A(u_{0}),x^{-})=H(A(u_{0}),x^{+}).

(13)

We next show that the map
z\mapsto H(A(u_{0}),z)
is strictly increasing on
[x^{-},x^{+}]
.
From (
13
) at
z=x^{-}
we get

\lambda x^{-}-A(u_{0})=\frac{Q(u_{0})-c^{\prime}(x^{-})}{c^{\prime\prime}(x^{-})}\geq 0,

because
Q(u_{0})\geq\phi^{*}(u_{0}^{-})=c^{\prime}(x^{-})
.
Hence for every
z\in[x^{-},x^{+}]
,
A(u_{0})\leq\lambda x^{-}\leq\lambda z
.
Therefore Lemma
10
implies that
z\longmapsto H(A(u_{0}),z)
is strictly increasing on
[x^{-},x^{+}]
.
This contradicts (
13
), since a strictly increasing function cannot take the same value at two distinct points.
Therefore
x^{-}=x^{+}
, and
x
is continuous at
u_{0}
.
∎

Therefore, the optimizer is characterized not only by a local first-order equation, but by an interior state system together with endpoint conditions that determine the stopping point
b
itself. We can now prove the fact that the Euler–Lagrange optimality condition reduces to a pointwise identity.

Corollary 8
.

The function
x
extends continuously to
[0,b]
by setting
x(b):=\bar{q}
.
Moreover,

Q(u)=H(A(u),x(u))=c^{\prime}(x(u))+c^{\prime\prime}(x(u))(\lambda x(u)-A(u))\quad\text{for every }u\in[0,b].

(14)

In particular,

A(u)\leq\lambda x(u)\qquad\text{for every }u\in[0,b].

(15)

Proof.

Continuity of
x
on
(0,b)
and continuity at
b
follow from Lemma
11
. Continuity at
0
follows from right-continuity of
\phi^{*}
and continuity of
q
.

Since
A
and
x
are continuous on
[0,b]
, the function
H
is continuous on
[0,b]
.
By (
9
), we have
H(u)=Q(u)
for a.e.
u\in(0,b)
.
Hence the continuous function
H-Q
vanishes a.e. on
(0,b)
, and therefore vanishes identically on
[0,b]
.
This proves (
14
).

Finally, since
Q(u)\geq\phi^{*}(u)=c^{\prime}(x(u))
for every
u\in[0,b]
, (
14
) implies

c^{\prime\prime}(x(u))(\lambda x(u)-A(u))=Q(u)-c^{\prime}(x(u))\geq 0
.
Because
c^{\prime\prime}(x(u))>0
for
u\in(0,b]
and the inequality is trivial at
u=0
(where
A(0)=0
), we obtain (
15
).
∎

The next step is to prove that
Q
is absolutely continuous on
[0,b]
.

Lemma 12
.

The function
Q
is absolutely continuous on
[0,b]
.
More precisely,

Q(u)=\frac{1}{1-u}\Bigl(\int_{u}^{b}\phi^{*}(t)\,dt+1-b\Bigr)\qquad(u\in[0,b]),

and for a.e.
u\in(0,b)
,

Q^{\prime}(u)=\frac{Q(u)-\phi^{*}(u)}{1-u}.

(16)

Proof.

For
u\in[0,b]
, since
\phi^{*}(t)=1
for all
t\in[b,1]
, we have

Q(u)=\frac{1}{1-u}\int_{u}^{1}\phi^{*}(t)\,dt=\frac{1}{1-u}\Bigl(\int_{u}^{b}\phi^{*}(t)\,dt+1-b\Bigr).

Define

F(u):=\int_{u}^{b}\phi^{*}(t)\,dt+1-b,\qquad u\in[0,b].

Because
\phi^{*}
is bounded on
[0,b]
, the function
F
is absolutely continuous on
[0,b]
and
F^{\prime}(u)=-\phi^{*}(u)
for a.e.
u\in(0,b)
. Since the function
u\mapsto(1-u)^{-1}
is
C^{1}
on
[0,b]
, the product/quotient rule for absolutely continuous functions yields that
Q(u)=F(u)/(1-u)
is absolutely continuous on
[0,b]
, and for a.e.
u\in(0,b)
,

Q^{\prime}(u)=\frac{F^{\prime}(u)(1-u)+F(u)}{(1-u)^{2}}=\frac{-\phi^{*}(u)(1-u)+F(u)}{(1-u)^{2}}=\frac{Q(u)-\phi^{*}(u)}{1-u}.

This proves (
16
).
∎

We proceed by showing that there exist open neighborhoods on which we can apply the Implicit Function Theorem. This will then be used in proving the absolute continuity of the optimal solution
\phi^{*}
.

Lemma 13

(Local implicit parameterization)
.

Fix
u_{0}\in(0,b]
and set

A_{0}:=A(u_{0})
,
x_{0}:=x(u_{0})
and
Q_{0}:=Q(u_{0})
. Then there exist neighborhoods
U_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}
of
A_{0}
,
V_{0}\subset\mathbb{R}
of
Q_{0}
,
I_{0}\subset(0,\infty)
of
x_{0}
, a relative open interval
J_{0}
of
u_{0}
in
(0,b]
, and a
C^{1}
map
X_{0}:U_{0}\times V_{0}\to I_{0}

such that:

(i)

for every
(A,Q)\in U_{0}\times V_{0}
,

H(A,X_{0}(A,Q))=Q
;

(ii)

for every
u\in J_{0}
,
x(u)=X_{0}(A(u),Q(u))
;

(iii)

there exists
L_{0}>0
such that

|x(u)-x(v)|\leq L_{0}\bigl(|A(u)-A(v)|+|Q(u)-Q(v)|\bigr)\qquad\text{for all }u,v\in J_{0}.

(17)

Proof.

If
u_{0}<b
, Corollary
7
gives
x_{0}=q(\phi^{*}(u_{0}))>0
. If
u_{0}=b
, Lemma
11
gives

x_{0}=x(b)=\bar{q}>0
. Thus
x_{0}>0
in all cases. By Corollary
8
,
H(A_{0},x_{0})=Q_{0}
and
\lambda x_{0}-A_{0}\geq 0
.
By Lemma
10
,

\partial_{z}H(A_{0},x_{0})=(1+\lambda)c^{\prime\prime}(x_{0})+(\lambda x_{0}-A_{0})c^{\prime\prime\prime}(x_{0})>0
.

Apply the implicit function theorem to

F(A,Q,z):=H(A,z)-Q
. It yields neighborhoods
U_{0}
of
A_{0}
,
V_{0}
of
Q_{0}
,
I_{0}
of
x_{0}
, and a unique

C^{1}
map
X_{0}:U_{0}\times V_{0}\to I_{0}
such that
H(A,X_{0}(A,Q))=Q
for all
(A,Q)\in U_{0}\times V_{0}
. This proves part (i).

Since
A
,
Q
, and
x
are continuous at
u_{0}
, after shrinking the neighborhoods if
necessary there exists a relative open interval
J_{0}\subset(0,b]
containing
u_{0}
such that
A(J_{0})\subset U_{0}
,
Q(J_{0})\subset V_{0}
, and
x(J_{0})\subset I_{0}
.
For every
u\in J_{0}
, Corollary
8
gives
H(A(u),x(u))=Q(u)
.
By uniqueness in the implicit function theorem,
x(u)=X_{0}(A(u),Q(u))
for all
u\in J_{0}
. This proves part (ii).

Finally,
X_{0}
is
C^{1}
, hence locally Lipschitz. Shrinking
J_{0}
once more so that

(A(J_{0}),Q(J_{0}))
has compact closure in
U_{0}\times V_{0}
, there exists
L_{0}>0
such
that
|x(u)-x(v)|\leq L_{0}\bigl(|A(u)-A(v)|+|Q(u)-Q(v)|\bigr)
for all
u,v\in J_{0}
.
This proves part (iii).
∎

This allows us to obtain:

Proposition 11

(Absolute continuity of the optimizer)
.

The function
x=q\circ\phi^{*}
is absolutely continuous on
[0,b]
.
Consequently,
\phi^{*}
is absolutely continuous on
[0,1]
.

Proof.

We first prove that
x
is absolutely continuous on every interval
[a,b]
with
a\in(0,b)
.
Fix such an
a
.
For each
u_{0}\in[a,b]
, Lemma
13
provides a relative open interval
J_{u_{0}}
containing
u_{0}
in
(0,b]
and a constant
L_{u_{0}}
such that

|x(u)-x(v)|\leq L_{u_{0}}\bigl(|A(u)-A(v)|+|Q(u)-Q(v)|\bigr)
for
u,v\in J_{u_{0}}
. The compact interval
[a,b]
is covered by finitely many such intervals,
[a,b]\subset J_{u_{1}}\cup\cdots\cup J_{u_{m}}
. Set

L_{*}:=\max\{L_{u_{1}},\dots,L_{u_{m}}\}
.
Because
A
and
Q
are absolutely continuous on
[0,b]
, there exists
\delta>0
such that for every finite disjoint family of intervals
\{(\alpha_{\ell},\beta_{\ell})\}_{\ell}
contained in
[a,b]
with total length less than
\delta
,

\sum_{\ell}|A(\beta_{\ell})-A(\alpha_{\ell})|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2L_{*}},\qquad\sum_{\ell}|Q(\beta_{\ell})-Q(\alpha_{\ell})|<\frac{\varepsilon}{2L_{*}}.

Now take any finite disjoint family of intervals
\{(a_{\ell},b_{\ell})\}_{\ell}
contained in
[a,b]
with total length less than
\delta
.
Refining the family by splitting at the finitely many endpoints of the intervals
J_{u_{j}}\cap[a,b]
if necessary, we may assume that each resulting subinterval lies inside one of the sets
J_{u_{j}}\cap[a,b]
.
Assign to each refined subinterval one index
j(\ell)
such that
(a_{\ell},b_{\ell})\subset J_{u_{j(\ell)}}\cap[a,b]
.
Then for each refined subinterval,

|x(b_{\ell})-x(a_{\ell})|\leq L_{u_{j(\ell)}}\bigl(|A(b_{\ell})-A(a_{\ell})|+|Q(b_{\ell})-Q(a_{\ell})|\bigr)\leq L_{*}\bigl(|A(b_{\ell})-A(a_{\ell})|+|Q(b_{\ell})-Q(a_{\ell})|\bigr).

Summing over all refined subintervals gives

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell}|x(b_{\ell})-x(a_{\ell})|

\displaystyle\leq L_{*}\sum_{\ell}\bigl(|A(b_{\ell})-A(a_{\ell})|+|Q(b_{\ell})-Q(a_{\ell})|\bigr)

\displaystyle<L_{*}\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{2L_{*}}+\frac{\varepsilon}{2L_{*}}\right)=\varepsilon.

Hence
x
is absolutely continuous on
[a,b]
.

We now extend this from
[a,b]
to
[0,b]
.
Since
x
is continuous at
0
, for every
\varepsilon>0
there exists
a\in(0,b)
such that
|x(a)-x(0)|<\varepsilon/2
.
Because
x
is absolutely continuous on
[a,b]
, there exists
\delta>0
such that for every finite disjoint family of intervals
\{(a_{\ell},b_{\ell})\}_{\ell}
contained in
[a,b]
with total length less than
\delta
,

\sum_{\ell}|x(b_{\ell})-x(a_{\ell})|<\varepsilon/2
.
Now take any finite disjoint family of intervals
\{(\alpha_{\ell},\beta_{\ell})\}_{\ell}
contained in
[0,b]
with total length less than
\delta
.
Splitting at the point
a
if necessary, we may assume that every interval in the family lies either in
[0,a]
or in
[a,b]
.
Since
x
is nondecreasing,

\sum_{\ell:\,(\alpha_{\ell},\beta_{\ell})\subset[0,a]}|x(\beta_{\ell})-x(\alpha_{\ell})|\leq x(a)-x(0)<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

The contribution of the intervals contained in
[a,b]
is less than
\varepsilon/2
by the choice of
\delta
.
Therefore,
\sum_{\ell}|x(\beta_{\ell})-x(\alpha_{\ell})|<\varepsilon
.
This proves that
x
is absolutely continuous on
[0,b]
.

Finally, Lemma
8
gives
\phi^{*}(0)>0
, hence
x(0)=q(\phi^{*}(0))>0
. Since
x
is continuous on
[0,b]
, there exists
\underline{x}>0
such that
x(u)\in[\underline{x},\bar{q}]
for all
u\in[0,b]
. Because
c^{\prime}
is
C^{1}
on
[\underline{x},\bar{q}]
, it is Lipschitz on that
interval. Therefore
\phi^{*}(u)=c^{\prime}(x(u))
,
u\in[0,b])
is absolutely continuous on
[0,b]
.
On
[b,1]
we have
\phi^{*}\equiv 1
, which is absolutely continuous.
Because Lemma
11
gives
\phi^{*}(b^{-})=1=\phi^{*}(b)
, the two pieces match continuously at
b
, and therefore
\phi^{*}
is absolutely continuous on
[0,1]
.
∎

We are now ready to derive in closed form the free-boundary system that pins down every solution to the upstream actor’s problem on
[0,b).

Proposition 12
.

Let
\phi^{*}
be a global maximizer, let
b
be its cutoff, and let
x
,
A
, and
Q
be as in (
6
).
Then:

(i)

x\in AC([0,b])
,
A\in C^{1}([0,b])
, and
Q\in AC([0,b])
;

(ii)

for every
u\in[0,b]
,

Q(u)=c^{\prime}(x(u))+c^{\prime\prime}(x(u))(\lambda x(u)-A(u));

(18)

(iii)

for a.e.
u\in(0,b)
,

\displaystyle A^{\prime}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{x(u)}{1-u},

(19)

\displaystyle Q^{\prime}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{Q(u)-c^{\prime}(x(u))}{1-u}=\frac{c^{\prime\prime}(x(u))(\lambda x(u)-A(u))}{1-u},

(20)

\displaystyle x^{\prime}(u)

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+\lambda)x(u)-A(u)}{(1-u)\left((1+\lambda)+(\lambda x(u)-A(u))\dfrac{c^{\prime\prime\prime}(x(u))}{c^{\prime\prime}(x(u))}\right)};

(21)

(iv)

the boundary conditions are

A(0)=0,\qquad x(b)=\bar{q},\qquad A(b)=\lambda\bar{q},\qquad Q(b)=1.

(22)

Proof.

Part (i) follows from Proposition
11
, the definition of
A
, and Lemma
12
.
Part (ii) is Corollary
8
.

For (
19
), note that the integrand
u\mapsto x(u)/(1-u)
is continuous on
[0,b]
because
x
is continuous and
b<1
.
Hence the fundamental theorem of calculus gives

A^{\prime}(u)=\frac{x(u)}{1-u}\qquad\text{for every }u\in[0,b].

For (
20
), use Lemma
12
together with (
18
).

To derive (
21
), differentiate (
18
) a.e. on
(0,b)
.
Since
x\in AC([0,b])
and
c\in C^{3}
, the chain rule gives

\displaystyle Q^{\prime}(u)

\displaystyle=c^{\prime\prime}(x(u))x^{\prime}(u)+c^{\prime\prime\prime}(x(u))x^{\prime}(u)(\lambda x(u)-A(u))+c^{\prime\prime}(x(u))(\lambda x^{\prime}(u)-A^{\prime}(u))

\displaystyle=\Bigl((1+\lambda)c^{\prime\prime}(x(u))+(\lambda x(u)-A(u))c^{\prime\prime\prime}(x(u))\Bigr)x^{\prime}(u)-c^{\prime\prime}(x(u))A^{\prime}(u).

Now substitute (
19
) and (
20
):

\frac{c^{\prime\prime}(x(u))(\lambda x(u)-A(u))}{1-u}=\Bigl((1+\lambda)c^{\prime\prime}(x(u))+(\lambda x(u)-A(u))c^{\prime\prime\prime}(x(u))\Bigr)x^{\prime}(u)-\frac{c^{\prime\prime}(x(u))x(u)}{1-u}.

Move the last term to the left-hand side:

\frac{c^{\prime\prime}(x(u))((1+\lambda)x(u)-A(u))}{1-u}=\Bigl((1+\lambda)c^{\prime\prime}(x(u))+(\lambda x(u)-A(u))c^{\prime\prime\prime}(x(u))\Bigr)x^{\prime}(u).

Because
x(u)>0
for every
u\in(0,b)
and (
15
) gives
A(u)\leq\lambda x(u)
for a.e.
u\in(0,b)
, Lemma
10
yields

(1+\lambda)c^{\prime\prime}(x(u))+(\lambda x(u)-A(u))c^{\prime\prime\prime}(x(u))>0\qquad\text{for a.e. }u\in(0,b).

Hence the coefficient of
x^{\prime}(u)
on the right-hand side is strictly positive for a.e.
u\in(0,b)
.
Dividing yields (
21
).

Finally, the boundary conditions (
22
) follow from the definitions of
A
and
Q
, together with Lemma
11
.
∎

Note that, equivalently, if one sets
T:=-\ln(1-b)
,
\widetilde{A}(t):=A(1-e^{-t})
,
\widetilde{Q}(t):=Q(1-e^{-t})
and
\widetilde{x}(t):=x(1-e^{-t})
,
then on
(0,T)
,

\displaystyle\dot{\widetilde{A}}(t)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{x}(t),

(23)

\displaystyle\dot{\widetilde{Q}}(t)

\displaystyle=c^{\prime\prime}(\widetilde{x}(t))(\lambda\widetilde{x}(t)-\widetilde{A}(t)),

(24)

\displaystyle\dot{\widetilde{x}}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{(1+\lambda)\widetilde{x}(t)-\widetilde{A}(t)}{(1+\lambda)+(\lambda\widetilde{x}(t)-\widetilde{A}(t))\dfrac{c^{\prime\prime\prime}(\widetilde{x}(t))}{c^{\prime\prime}(\widetilde{x}(t))}},

(25)

and the boundary conditions become

\widetilde{A}(T)=\lambda\bar{q}
,
\widetilde{x}(T)=\bar{q}
and
\widetilde{Q}(T)=1
.

Finally, we can now state and prove that the solution to the upstream actor’s problem is unique.

Proposition 13
.

For every
k\in(1/2,1]
, the problem has a unique global maximizer.
In particular, the optimizer free-boundary system of Proposition
12
admits at most one solution arising from a global maximizer.

Proof.

Let
\phi_{1}^{*}
and
\phi_{2}^{*}
be two global maximizers. For
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
b_{i}
be the cutoff
of
\phi_{i}^{*}
, and let
x_{i}
,
A_{i}
, and
Q_{i}
be the associated functions from Proposition
12
.
Define
T_{i}:=-\ln(1-b_{i})>0
. For
t\in[0,T_{i}]
, set
\widetilde{A}_{i}(t):=A_{i}(1-e^{-t})
,
\widetilde{Q}_{i}(t):=Q_{i}(1-e^{-t})
,
and
\widetilde{x}_{i}(t):=x_{i}(1-e^{-t})
.Then Proposition
12
implies that for a.e.
t\in(0,T_{i})
,
\widetilde{A}_{i}(t)

\widetilde{x}_{i}(t)
and
\widetilde{Q}_{i}(t)
are given by equations
23
-
25

with terminal condition

\widetilde{A}_{i}(T_{i})=\lambda\bar{q}
,
\widetilde{Q}_{i}(T_{i})=1
and
\widetilde{x}_{i}(T_{i})=\bar{q}
.

Reverse time by defining, for
s\in[0,T_{i}]
,

\widehat{A}_{i}(s):=\widetilde{A}_{i}(T_{i}-s)
,
\widehat{Q}_{i}(s):=\widetilde{Q}_{i}(T_{i}-s)
and
x_{i}(s):=\widetilde{x}_{i}(T_{i}-s)
.
Then for a.e.
s\in(0,T_{i})
,

\widehat{A}_{i}^{\prime}(s)=-\widehat{x}_{i}(s),\qquad\widehat{Q}_{i}^{\prime}(s)=-c^{\prime\prime}(\widehat{x}_{i}(s))\bigl(\lambda\widehat{x}_{i}(s)-\widehat{A}_{i}(s)\bigr),\qquad\widehat{Q}_{i}(s)=H(\widehat{A}_{i}(s),\widehat{x}_{i}(s)),

and
\widehat{A}_{i}(0)=\lambda\bar{q}
,
\widehat{Q}_{i}(0)=1
and
\widehat{x}_{i}(0)=\bar{q}
. Thus, the two reverse-time trajectories start from the same point.

We first prove that the two reverse-time state trajectories coincide on the common time interval.
Set
S^{*}:=\min\{T_{1},T_{2}\}
, and define

S:=\Bigl\{s\in[0,S^{*}]:\widehat{A}_{1}(r)=\widehat{A}_{2}(r),\ \widehat{Q}_{1}(r)=\widehat{Q}_{2}(r)\ \text{for every }r\in[0,s]\Bigr\}.

By the common initial condition,
0\in S
, so
S
is nonempty. Let
s^{*}:=\sup\mathcal{S}
. Since
\widehat{A}_{i},\widehat{Q}_{i}
are continuous,
\mathcal{S}
is closed under increasing limits. Hence
s^{*}\in\mathcal{S}
, and the two state paths coincide on
[0,s^{*}]
. We claim that
s^{*}=S^{*}
. Suppose instead that
s^{*}<S^{*}
. At
s^{*}
, write

A^{*}:=\widehat{A}_{1}(s^{*})=\widehat{A}_{2}(s^{*})
and
Q^{*}:=\widehat{Q}_{1}(s^{*})=\widehat{Q}_{2}(s^{*})
.
Both
\widehat{x}_{1}(s^{*})
and
\widehat{x}_{2}(s^{*})
solve
Q^{*}=H(A^{*},x)
,
and both satisfy
A^{*}\leq\lambda x
. By Lemma 10, this solution is unique, so

\widehat{x}_{1}(s^{*})=\widehat{x}_{2}(s^{*})
.
By Lemma
13
, in a neighborhood of
(A^{*},Q^{*})
there is a
C^{1}
function
X
such that

\widehat{x}_{i}(s)=X(\widehat{A}_{i}(s),\widehat{Q}_{i}(s))
.
Therefore, near
s^{*}
, both pairs
(\widehat{A}_{i},\widehat{Q}_{i})
solve the same locally Lipschitz ODE with the same initial condition at
s^{*}
. Local uniqueness for ODEs implies that the two paths coincide on
[s^{*},s^{*}+\eta]\cap[0,S^{*}]
for some
\eta>0
, contradicting the definition of
s^{*}
. Hence
s^{*}=S^{*}
and we have proved
\widehat{A}_{1}(s)=\widehat{A}_{2}(s)
,
\widehat{Q}_{1}(s)=\widehat{Q}_{2}(s)
for all
s\in[0,S^{*}]
.

We now prove that
T_{1}=T_{2}
. Suppose, for contradiction, that
T_{1}<T_{2}
. Then
S^{*}=T_{1}
,
so by the equality just established,
\widehat{A}_{1}(T_{1})=\widehat{A}_{2}(T_{1})
.
But by definition of
\widehat{A}_{1}
,
\widehat{A}_{1}(T_{1})=\widetilde{A}_{1}(0)=A_{1}(0)=0
. On the other hand,

\widehat{A}_{2}(T_{1})=\widetilde{A}_{2}(T_{2}-T_{1})=A_{2}\bigl(1-e^{-(T_{2}-T_{1})}\bigr).

Since
T_{2}-T_{1}>0
, the argument of
A_{2}
belongs to
(0,b_{2})
. Because
x_{2}(u)>0
for every

u\in(0,b_{2})
,

A_{2}\bigl(1-e^{-(T_{2}-T_{1})}\bigr)=\int_{0}^{1-e^{-(T_{2}-T_{1})}}\frac{x_{2}(s)}{1-s}\,ds>0.

This contradicts
\widehat{A}_{1}(T_{1})=\widehat{A}_{2}(T_{1})
. Therefore,
T_{1}=T_{2}
,
b_{1}=b_{2}
.

Since
T_{1}=T_{2}
, the equality of the reverse-time states holds on the whole interval
[0,T_{1}]
.
Reversing the time change gives
A_{1}(u)=A_{2}(u)
and
Q_{1}(u)=Q_{2}(u)
for all
u\in[0,b_{1}]
.

We next prove equality of the quality paths. Fix
u\in(0,b_{1}]
. Then
x_{i}(u)>0
for both
i
:
if
u<b_{1}
, this follows from Corollary
7
, while if
u=b_{1}
, Proposition
12
gives
x_{i}(b_{1})=\bar{q}
. Since
A_{1}(u)=A_{2}(u)
,
Q_{1}(u)=Q_{2}(u)
,
both
x_{1}(u)
and
x_{2}(u)
solve
H(A_{1}(u),z)=Q_{1}(u)
. Also, by
15
,
A_{1}(u)\leq\lambda x_{i}(u)
,
(i=1,2)
.
Assume without loss of generality that
x_{1}(u)\leq x_{2}(u)
. Then for every
z\in[x_{1}(u),x_{2}(u)]
we have
A_{1}(u)\leq\lambda x_{1}(u)\leq\lambda z
.
Hence Lemma
10
implies that
z\mapsto H(A_{1}(u),z)
is strictly increasing on
[x_{1}(u),x_{2}(u)]
. Since it takes the same value
Q_{1}(u)
at both endpoints, we must have
x_{1}(u)=x_{2}(u)
for all
u\in(0,b_{1}]
. Because both
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
are continuous on
[0,b_{1}]
, letting
u\downarrow 0
gives
x_{1}(0)=x_{2}(0)
. Thus,
x_{1}(u)=x_{2}(u)
for all
u\in[0,b_{1}]
.

Finally,
\phi_{1}^{*}(u)=c^{\prime}(x_{1}(u))=c^{\prime}(x_{2}(u))=\phi_{2}^{*}(u)
for all
u\in[0,b_{1}]
, and both profiles equal
1
on
[b_{1},1]
. Therefore,
\phi_{1}^{*}\equiv\phi_{2}^{*}
. This proves uniqueness of the optimal virtual value profile.
∎

Finally, we have:

Lemma 14
.

Fix
k>1/2
, and suppose Assumption
2
holds. Let
\phi^{*}\in\Phi
be the unique maximizer of
\max_{\phi\in\Phi}J_{k}(\phi)
.
Then
G_{\phi^{*}}
is the unique maximizer of the original problem
\max_{G\in\Delta([0,1])}W_{k}(G)
.

Proof.

By Lemmas
1
and
2
,
G_{\phi^{*}}
is an optimizer of the original problem. We prove uniqueness.

Let
G\in\Delta([0,1])
be any optimizer of the original problem. Let
Q
be its lower quantile, let
\widehat{R}(u)=(1-u)Q(u)
, let
R=\operatorname{cav}(\widehat{R})
, and let
\phi
be the associated ironed virtual value. Let
\widetilde{G}
be the regularization from Lemma
1
, with quantile

\widetilde{Q}(u)=\frac{R(u)}{1-u}.

Since
G
is optimal and Lemma
1
gives
W_{k}(\widetilde{G})\geq W_{k}(G)
,
\widetilde{G}
is also optimal. Because
k>0
, the equality condition in Lemma
1
gives

\bigl(\widetilde{Q}(u)-Q(u)\bigr)q(\phi(u))=0
for a.e.
u\in(0,1)
.

Now apply Lemma
2
to
\widetilde{G}
. The resulting distribution has virtual value
\phi^{+}=\max\{\phi,0\}
and attains the same value as
\widetilde{G}
. Hence
\phi^{+}
is a maximizer of the reduced problem. By uniqueness of the reduced maximizer,

\phi^{+}=\phi^{*}
.
By Lemma
5
,
\phi^{*}(u)>0
for every
u\in(0,1)
. Hence

\phi(u)=\phi^{*}(u)
for every
u\in(0,1)
.
The pointwise seller problem then implies

q(\phi(u))=q(\phi^{*}(u))>0
for every
u\in(0,1)
. Therefore, the equality condition from Lemma
1
yields
Q(u)=\widetilde{Q}(u)
for a.e.
u\in(0,1)
. But

\widetilde{Q}(u)=\frac{R(u)}{1-u}=\frac{1}{1-u}\int_{u}^{1}\phi(t)\,dt=\frac{1}{1-u}\int_{u}^{1}\phi^{*}(t)\,dt=Q_{\phi^{*}}(u)

for every
u\in(0,1)
. Hence

Q(u)=Q_{\phi^{*}}(u)
for a.e.
u\in(0,1)
.
Since a distribution is the law of its quantile evaluated at a uniform random variable, equality of lower quantiles almost everywhere implies equality of the corresponding distributions. Thus

G=G_{\phi^{*}}
.
Therefore,
G_{\phi^{*}}
is the unique optimizer of the original problem.
∎

This completes the proof of Theorem
1
. ∎

Proof of Corollary
1

Proof.

By Corollary
7
,
\phi^{*}
is strictly increasing on
(0,b)
, satisfies
0<\phi^{*}(u)<1
for all

u\in(0,b)
, and
\phi^{*}(u)=1
for all
u\in[b,1]
.

First,

\underline{v}=Q^{*}(0)=\int_{0}^{1}\phi^{*}(t)\,dt=(1-b)+\int_{0}^{b}\phi^{*}(t)\,dt=1-\int_{0}^{b}(1-\phi^{*}(t))\,dt.

Since
\phi^{*}(t)=1
on
[b,1]
, we have
\underline{v}\geq\int_{b}^{1}1\,dt=1-b>0
.
Since
b>0
and
\phi^{*}(t)<1
on
(0,b)
, it also follows that
\underline{v}<(1-b)+\int_{0}^{b}1\,dt=1
. Hence
0<\underline{v}<1
.

Next, let
0\leq u<v<b
. Then

(1-u)Q^{*}(u)=\int_{u}^{v}\phi^{*}(t)\,dt+(1-v)Q^{*}(v).

Because
\phi^{*}
is strictly increasing on
(0,b)
, we have
\phi^{*}(t)<\phi^{*}(v)
for every

t\in[u,v)
, and since
Q^{*}(v)
is the average of
\phi^{*}
on
[v,1]
, we have
\phi^{*}(v)\leq Q^{*}(v)
.
Therefore,
\int_{u}^{v}\phi^{*}(t)\,dt<(v-u)Q^{*}(v)
, which implies
(1-u)Q^{*}(u)<(v-u)Q^{*}(v)+(1-v)Q^{*}(v)=(1-u)Q^{*}(v)
.
Hence
Q^{*}(u)<Q^{*}(v)
, so
Q^{*}
is strictly increasing on
[0,b)
.
If instead
u\in[b,1]
, then
\phi^{*}(t)=1
for all
t\in[u,1]
, so

Q^{*}(u)=\frac{1}{1-u}\int_{u}^{1}1\,dt=1.

Thus
Q^{*}
is constant and equal to
1
on
[b,1]
.

Moreover,
Q^{*}
is continuous on
[0,1)
. Since it is strictly increasing on
[0,b)
and

Q^{*}(b)=1
, its range is exactly
[\underline{v},1]
. Because
G^{*}
is the law of
Q^{*}(U)
with

U\sim U[0,1]
, it follows that
\operatorname{supp}(G^{*})=[\underline{v},1]
.
Finally, for any
v\in[\underline{v},1)
, strict increase of
Q^{*}
on
[0,b)
implies that the set
\{u\in[0,1]:Q^{*}(u)=v\}
, has zero Lebesgue measure so
G^{*}
has no atom at any
v<1
. On the other hand,
\{u\in[0,1]:Q^{*}(u)=1\}=[b,1]
, hence
G^{*}(\{1\})=\lambda([b,1])=1-b
. So
G^{*}
has a unique atom at
v=1
of size
1-b
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
1

Proof.

Let
\lambda_{i}:=\frac{2k_{i}-1}{k_{i}}
,
i=1,2
.
Since
k_{2}>k_{1}>1/2
, we have
\lambda_{2}>\lambda_{1}
.

For each
i
, Proposition
12
gives a cutoff
b_{i}\in(0,1)
, an interior quality schedule
x_{i}=q(\phi_{i})
, and functions
A_{i},Q_{i}
on
[0,b_{i}]
satisfying

Q_{i}(u)=c^{\prime}(x_{i}(u))+c^{\prime\prime}(x_{i}(u))\bigl(\lambda_{i}x_{i}(u)-A_{i}(u)\bigr),

A_{i}^{\prime}(u)=\frac{x_{i}(u)}{1-u},\qquad Q_{i}^{\prime}(u)=\frac{Q_{i}(u)-c^{\prime}(x_{i}(u))}{1-u},

with
x_{i}(b_{i})=\bar{q}
,
A_{i}(b_{i})=\lambda_{i}\bar{q}
and
Q_{i}(b_{i})=1
. Moreover,
Q_{i}(u)>c^{\prime}(x_{i}(u))
for all
u\in(0,b_{i})
, because the interior is strictly separating and the top tail is efficient.

As in the uniqueness proof, pass to log time
t=-\ln(1-u)
, invert the strictly increasing map
\widetilde{A}_{i}(t):=A_{i}(1-e^{-t})
, and define
s_{i}:[0,\lambda_{i}\bar{q}]\to[0,T_{i}]
and
T_{i}:=-\ln(1-b_{i})
, together with
X_{i}(A):=\widetilde{x}_{i}(s_{i}(A))
and
\mathcal{Q}_{i}(A):=\widetilde{Q}_{i}(s_{i}(A))
.
Then
X_{i}
is strictly increasing,
\mathcal{Q}_{i}
is
C^{1}
, and
\mathcal{Q}_{i}^{\prime}(A)=R_{\lambda_{i}}(A,\mathcal{Q}_{i}(A))
,
where
X_{\lambda}(A,Q)
is the unique solution of

Q=c^{\prime}(x)+c^{\prime\prime}(x)(\lambda x-A)
,
x\in[A/\lambda,\bar{q}]
,
and

R_{\lambda}(A,Q):=\frac{Q-c^{\prime}(X_{\lambda}(A,Q))}{X_{\lambda}(A,Q)}.

Note that
X_{\lambda}(A,Q)
is well defined on the domain used below. To see this, recall that

H_{\lambda}(A,x)=c^{\prime}(x)+c^{\prime\prime}(x)(\lambda x-A)
.
If
0\leq A\leq\lambda\bar{q}
and

c^{\prime}(A/\lambda)\leq Q\leq 1
,
then
X_{\lambda}(A,Q)
exists and is unique in
[A/\lambda,\bar{q}]
. Indeed,

H_{\lambda}(A,A/\lambda)=c^{\prime}(A/\lambda)\leq Q
,
while
H_{\lambda}(A,\bar{q})=1+c^{\prime\prime}(\bar{q})(\lambda\bar{q}-A)\geq 1\geq Q
.
Continuity gives existence. Uniqueness follows from Lemma
10
, since
A\leq\lambda x
on
[A/\lambda,\bar{q}]
.
In particular, if
\lambda_{2}>\lambda_{1}
,
A\in[0,\lambda_{1}\bar{q}]
, and
Q_{1}(A)
is generated by the
\lambda_{1}
-solution, then
X_{\lambda_{2}}(A,Q_{1}(A))
is well defined: indeed,

c^{\prime}(A/\lambda_{2})\leq c^{\prime}(A/\lambda_{1})\leq Q_{1}(A)\leq 1
.

We now record the monotonicity properties of
X_{\lambda}
and
R_{\lambda}
used below.
All comparisons below are on
A\in(0,\lambda_{1}\bar{q}]
, so
A>0
.
Lemma
10

implies that
x\mapsto H_{\lambda}(A,x)

is strictly increasing on
[A/\lambda,\bar{q}]
.
It follows first that
X_{\lambda}(A,Q)
is strictly increasing in
Q
. Indeed, if

Q^{\prime}<Q^{\prime\prime}
,
x^{\prime}=X_{\lambda}(A,Q^{\prime})
, and
x^{\prime\prime}=X_{\lambda}(A,Q^{\prime\prime})
, then
H_{\lambda}(A,x^{\prime})=Q^{\prime}<Q^{\prime\prime}=H_{\lambda}(A,x^{\prime\prime})
,
so strict monotonicity of
H_{\lambda}(A,\cdot)
gives
x^{\prime}<x^{\prime\prime}
.

Next fix
A>0
and
Q
, and let
0<\lambda^{\prime}<\lambda^{\prime\prime}
. Suppose
x^{\prime}=X_{\lambda^{\prime}}(A,Q)
and
x^{\prime\prime}=X_{\lambda^{\prime\prime}}(A,Q)
exist. Since
x^{\prime}\geq A/\lambda^{\prime}>A/\lambda^{\prime\prime}
, the point
x^{\prime}
belongs to the relevant domain for

\lambda^{\prime\prime}
. Moreover,

H_{\lambda^{\prime\prime}}(A,x^{\prime})=H_{\lambda^{\prime}}(A,x^{\prime})+(\lambda^{\prime\prime}-\lambda^{\prime})x^{\prime}c^{\prime\prime}(x^{\prime})=Q+(\lambda^{\prime\prime}-\lambda^{\prime})x^{\prime}c^{\prime\prime}(x^{\prime})>Q.

Since
H_{\lambda^{\prime\prime}}(A,\cdot)
is strictly increasing and
H_{\lambda^{\prime\prime}}(A,x^{\prime\prime})=Q
,
we obtain
x^{\prime\prime}<x^{\prime}
.
Thus
X_{\lambda}(A,Q)
is strictly decreasing in
\lambda
on the region used below.

Finally, we prove the induced monotonicity of

R_{\lambda}(A,Q):=\frac{Q-c^{\prime}(X_{\lambda}(A,Q))}{X_{\lambda}(A,Q)}.

For fixed
A,Q
, define

r(x):=\frac{Q-c^{\prime}(x)}{x}.

Let
x^{\prime}=X_{\lambda^{\prime}}(A,Q)
. Since

Q=c^{\prime}(x^{\prime})+c^{\prime\prime}(x^{\prime})(\lambda^{\prime}x^{\prime}-A)

and
A\leq\lambda^{\prime}x^{\prime}
, we have
Q\geq c^{\prime}(x^{\prime})
. Hence, for every
x\in(0,x^{\prime}]
,

Q-c^{\prime}(x)\geq Q-c^{\prime}(x^{\prime})\geq 0
.
Therefore

r^{\prime}(x)=-\frac{xc^{\prime\prime}(x)+Q-c^{\prime}(x)}{x^{2}}<0\qquad\forall x\in(0,x^{\prime}].

From the previous paragraph,
X_{\lambda^{\prime\prime}}(A,Q)<X_{\lambda^{\prime}}(A,Q)=x^{\prime}
. Hence

R_{\lambda^{\prime\prime}}(A,Q)=r(X_{\lambda^{\prime\prime}}(A,Q))>r(X_{\lambda^{\prime}}(A,Q))=R_{\lambda^{\prime}}(A,Q).

Thus
R_{\lambda}(A,Q)
is strictly increasing in
\lambda
on the relevant region.

We first compare
\mathcal{Q}_{1}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
on the common
A
-interval
(0,\lambda_{1}\bar{q}]
. Since
\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\lambda_{1}\bar{q})=1
and
\mathcal{Q}_{2}(\lambda_{2}\bar{q})=1
, while
\mathcal{Q}_{2}
is strictly increasing, we have

\mathcal{Q}_{2}(\lambda_{1}\bar{q})<1=\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\lambda_{1}\bar{q})
. Suppose there were
A_{0}\in(0,\lambda_{1}\bar{q}]
with
